# 1: HOW TO PLAY

Participants in BEACON take on one of two roles: either that of a **player** or that of the **game master** (**GM**).

The goal of all participants is to tell a shared story, and to have fun doing so. Each player creates a single **player character** (**PC**) – a heroic figure called a "**beacon**" – and explores the game through that character's story. The GM, on the other hand, controls all of the **non-player characters** (**NPCs**), sets up combat scenes, and helps shape the narrative.

## CORE PRINCIPLES

BEACON has six core principles:

- 1 The well-being and fun of the group, including both the players and the GM, is the top priority.
- 2 Specific text takes precedent over general text.
- 3 BEACON does not model reality or real-life physics, and is not meant to.
- 4 Narrative play and combat play are distinct modes of play. The ability to do something in one does not automatically grant the ability to do it in the other.
- 5 In combat play, players can do anything their abilities allow, even if it doesn't make narrative sense.
- 6 Always round up to the nearest whole number.

#### GENERAL TEXT VS. SPECIFIC TEXT

General text includes basic rules of the game, such as "The Fight action is used to make a melee or improvised weapon attack."

Specific text is more granular or detailed than general text. For example, the Slow tag specifies that, "Abilities with this tag can **only** be activated as part of Full Attack." This means that a Slow weapon can't be used as part of the Fight action, because the text of the Slow tag is more specific than the text of the Fight action.

### COMBAT AND NARRATIVE SCENES

BEACON'sgameplay is split into **scenes**. A scene is a series of events that focus on a person, place, or challenge. Each scene is either a **narrative scene** or a **combat scene**, falling under the rules of either **combat play** or **narrative play**.

The GM determines when one scene ends and another begins, and whether the scenes are narrative or combat. But anyone can suggest or request a scene, and you are encouraged to do so.

Combat scenes use the rules for combat play ([p.](#page-40-0) [41\)](#page-40-0). These scenes feature important fights and battles. Not every fight is worthy of a combat scene; only those that are important or tactically challenging move into combat play. A simple street fight or barroom brawl, for example, wouldn't start a combat scene. A fight on a crumbling bridge against a horde of demons, or a climactic showdown with an archnemesis would be worthy of a combat scene.

*Example:* An angry necromancer has performed a ritual in a nearby cemetery, creating a continuous stream of undead soldiers that attack nearby towns. The beacons are asked to stop the necromancer and break the ritual. The GM sets up a combat scene with the beacons fighting in a misty cemetery against undead hordes and a necromancer riding an animated mausoleum!

Narrative scenes, on the other hand, use the rules for narrative play [\(p. 22](#page-21-0)). Any scene that isn't a combat scene is considered a narrative scene. These scenes involve challenges that are resolved by rolling dice (called "skill checks") or roleplaying. Small-scale fights can even happen during narrative play, but these fights are resolved just like any other challenge that happens during narrative play, using skill checks and roleplaying instead of the combat play rules.

*Example:* The beacons need to get to a floating island and the only way to get there is with an airship. They don't have one, so they ask the GM for a scene with the Artificer's Guild. The GM sets up a scene in which the beacons meet the guildmaster directly. The beacons attempt to convince the guildmaster to let them borrow an airship, using a mix of roleplaying and skill checks to determine how successful their attempts are.

### STRUCTURE OF PLAY

The gameplay of BEACON revolves around **quests**. Quests are missions, tasks, or jobs provided to the beacons by the GM. Quests contain both combat and narrative scenes, connecting them to tell stories. The quests that your characters participate in form the body of your **campaign**, which is the overarching story of your characters.

For example, your group's primary motivation might be to defeat a great evil. In order to gain the strength, allies, and resources to do so, they might need to undertake a number of quests, preparing themselves for the final showdown with their foe.

The time between quests uses the rules for narrative play. During this time, the players collaboratively tell their beacons' stories and explore the world.

When the players are ready to begin a new quest, they equip their beacons with abilities they've unlocked that they think will best suit the upcoming quest and the combat scenes within it.

When the beacons return from a quest, they **level up** [\(p. 14\)](#page-13-0) and become more powerful. This allows them to combine abilities and equipment from multiple **classes** [\(p. 84\)](#page-83-0) to create unique playstyles and character configurations.

### DICE

During both narrative play and combat, players roll dice to determine success, failure, and other interesting outcomes.

BEACON uses two types of dice: **20-sided dice** (**d20**) and **6-sided dice** (**d6**). The rules sometimes call for a **d3**, which can be represented with a d6. A result of 1–2 equals 1, 3–4 equals 2, and 5–6 equals 3.

A number in front of the type of die indicates how many dice of that type to roll. 3d6 means you roll 3 six-sided dice.

### ATTACKS, CHECKS, AND SAVES

There are three main types of dice rolls in BEACON: **attack rolls**, **checks**, and **saves**. They each use a single d20. Any time you're making one of these rolls, roll a d20 and add the appropriate **modifiers** to the result – bonuses or penalties to the roll based on beneficial or disadvantageous situations. The final result is your **total** for the roll.

#### ATTACKS

**Attacks** are hostile actions taken against other characters in combat play. They are usually made with weapons, spells, or skills. When attacking, you add your beacon's Grit score to the roll. See **Chapter 5: Combat Play** ([p.](#page-40-0) [41\)](#page-40-0) for more information.

#### CHECKS

**Checks** are rolls to determine how successful challenging actions are. There are two types of checks that you might make: **skill checks** and **ability checks**. To make a check of either type, you roll a d20 and add any relevant modifiers. To succeed, you need a total of **10 or more** (after modifiers).

**Skill checks** are used in narrative play. If one of a character's **background skills** ([p. 12\)](#page-11-0) would be helpful for the roll they're making, or they can invoke their **title** [\(p. 11](#page-10-0)), they can get a bonus on to the roll. See **Chapter 4: Narrative Play** ([p. 22](#page-21-0)) for more information.

**Ability checks**, by contrast, are used in combat play. They use the character's relevant **ability score** – Bulk, Agility, Mind, or Magic – as a modifier [\(p. 49](#page-48-0)).

During combat play, characters sometimes need to make **contested checks**. In a contested check, the two relevant parties each roll an ability check. Whoever gets the highest total succeeds, with ties going to the one who attacked or initiated the action.

#### SAVES

**Saves** are rolls made to resist effects during combat play. Effects that prompt a save indicate which of the target's ability scores to use as a modifier. Each character has a Save Target, which is the total their opponents must meet or exceed when rolling saves. When there is no Save Target indicated, it defaults to **10**.

### MODIFIERS

There are three types of modifiers that can affect rolls, increasing or decreasing their results:

- Ø Accuracy ( )
- Ø Difficulty ( )
- Ø Static modifiers (+1, –4, etc.).

Bonuses from your beacon's ability scores and Grit count as static modifiers.

#### ACCURACY AND DIFFICULTY

Various effects can apply Accuracy or Difficulty to a d20 roll.

For each or , roll 1d6 and take the **single highest result** out of the dice rolled. If rolling with , this number is added to the total as a **positive modifier**. If rolling with , it is subtracted from the total as a **negative modifier**. For example, if you are rolling with **2** , you would roll 2d6 and keep the single highest result out of all the dice. If you rolled a 2 and a 4, you would keep the 4.

Rolls can be affected by multiple instances of or , although they cancel each other out on a one-toone basis. For example, a roll could receive **4** and **2** ; however, the **2**  would cancel out **2** , leaving the roll with **2** . Always determine the final amount of or before rolling any dice.

An individual roll can't benefit from more than **6** or .

# 2: ANATOMY OF A BEACON

This chapter walks through the steps of creating a character and outlines the various components that define them.

In BEACON, the characters created and portrayed by the players are special individuals known as "beacons". They are called this because of the way they act like shining lights to others. There is no one way that beacons act or conduct themselves; no matter what, they always find themselves in positions where they can inspire or help others.

The precise characteristics that make someone a beacon differ between Reflections. In one Reflection, beacons might be mystically empowered individuals born under a strange star. In another, they might be

Beacons don't need a reason to be special in your game. The fact they are controlled by a player already makes them special, and grants them the opportunity to influence and change the story as it's told. Your game could be about a band of heroes destined to save the world, or it could be about a found family, fighting to keep itself safe.

The only constant across all Reflections is that beacons inspire people around them. Even those who are cowardly or timid have the potential and opportunity, as a group, to give hope to others.

**Chapter 10: Game Master's Guide** in the full version of BEACON details some example Reflections and the roles of beacons within them.

### CHARACTER CREATION

This section will guide you through the steps of creating a beacon.

Every beacon has a **character level** [\(p. 14\)](#page-13-0). Character levels represent the overall growth and power of a beacon. Higher-level beacons have more abilities available to them, and can therefore handle a wider variety of situations.

Your beacon begins at **character level 1**. At level 1, they have:

- Ø a name and a title
- Ø three rank 1 background skills ([p. 12\)](#page-11-0)
- Ø 2 ability points ([p. 12\)](#page-11-1)
- Ø three rank 1 talents ([p. 13\)](#page-12-0)
- Ø an ancestry [\(p. 13](#page-12-1))
- Ø one class rank ([p. 13\)](#page-12-2)
- Ø access to all basic equipment and techniques [\(p. 13](#page-12-3))
- Ø an appearance [\(p. 13](#page-12-4)).

Once you have finished these steps, you will determine your beacon's attributes, which are values used in combat play [\(p. 14](#page-13-1)).

#### NAME

#### Choose a name for your beacon

Beacons can have any sort of name, from the mundane to the grandiose. Alternatively, you can decide this later. You might not have an idea for a name yet, but might think of one after picking your background skills or ancestry. There are also plenty of random name generators online that you can use for inspiration.

*Example:* Shawn is creating her first beacon. She likes the name Dhalia, so she uses it for her character.

#### <span id="page-10-0"></span>TITLE

#### Choose a title for your beacon

When the stories of your character and their fellow beacons become legend, how will they be remembered? What will they be called when their name falls into obscurity and all that survives is their deeds?

People recognize your beacon by this title, even if they don't know their name. Titles can reference anything from a fighting style to a physical trait or even an aesthetic.

*Example titles:* The Green Knight, the Herald of Silence, the Bosstown Bludgeon, Moonfang, the Timeless Bard.

Whenever you make a skill check during narrative play, you can invoke your beacon's title before rolling to gain a **+2 bonus**. To invoke a title, the action in question must be something that contributes to the legend of your character's title. Think of a story being told about your beacon, their name lost to history and only their title remaining. Would this make it into the story?

*Example:* Shawn likes the idea of playing someone smart; a knowledge-seeker. She gives Dhalia the title of "the All-Knowing". Shawn can invoke this title when doing something that contributes to the legacy of "the All-Knowing" beacon. For example, if Dhalia is making a skill check to piece together the conspiracy behind the Day of Shadows, she can invoke her title to gain a +2 bonus to the roll. If Dhalia is making a skill check to climb a mountain, on the other hand, being all-knowing is less likely to be helpful, and couldn't be invoked.

The GM decides whether a title can be invoked or not. Players are encouraged to find creative explanations for why a beacon's title might apply, but they're not meant to be used in every situation.

If you find that your title is too easy to invoke, consider rephrasing it to limit its scope.

*Example:* Delmar's beacon is Dmitri, "the Demigod". After playing a few sessions, he realizes that "the Demigod" can be invoked in every situation. Delmar rephrases the title as "the Demigod of Determination", which puts some limits on when it can be invoked.

#### WRITING YOUR LEGEND

One way you can use titles is as a way of documenting the legend of your beacon. Whenever you invoke their title, write down what it was being used for and what the outcome was. When you're finished playing that character, look over everything that was written down. This is the foundation for the legends told about your beacon for generations to come.

#### <span id="page-11-0"></span>BACKGROUND SKILLS

#### At level 1, create three rank 1 background skills

**Background skills** are words or brief phrases that represent a beacon's history and training, and provide bonuses to skill checks. Background skills have three ranks. When you make a skill check in narrative play and one of your background skills is relevant, you get **+1**  on your roll for each rank you have in that skill. Each skill check can only benefit from one background skill, which must be decided before rolling.

Background skills can be anything, so make sure they're evocative. Instead of just "**Acrobat**", make it something interesting like, "**Acrobat Performer for the Demon Circus**".

*Example background skills:* Will of the Source, Troll-Wrestling Champion, An Eye for Lies, Royal Assassin.

When creating a background skill, think about what situations it might be relevant in. If it seems like it would apply to every skill check you might make, you should narrow its focus more.

Every time your beacon levels up they get a background skill point. These points can be used to either increase the rank of an existing background skill (to a maximum of **rank 3**), or create a new background skill at rank 1.

*Example:* Shawn creates a background skill "Truthseeker" for Dhalia. This can be used any time Dhalia is trying to learn the truth about something, whether researching in books or detecting lies.

Shawn also thinks that Dhalia should know lightbased magic, so she creates the background skill "Disciple of the Sun", which would apply whenever she uses magic.

Finally, Shawn creates the background skill "Multipurpose Clothing" because she likes the idea of Dhalia having clothes with secondary benefits, like a cloak that can be used as a paraglider. She would apply this background skill whenever she can think of a fun way to solve a problem with clothes.

#### <span id="page-11-1"></span>ABILITY SCORES

#### At level 1, assign 2 ability points

Your beacon has four **ability scores** that affect their attributes and actions in combat play:

- Ø Bulk
- Ø Mind
- Ø Agility
- Ø Magic

Ability scores range from **+0** to **+6**, and give bonuses in combat play. By default, they start at **+0**.

Each ability score also provides bonuses to some of your character's other attributes ([p. 14\)](#page-13-1). For example, Agility affects the character's Dodge and Speed.

Assigning an ability point to an ability score increases it by +1. At level 1, this means you can have two ability scores at +1 each, or one ability score at +2.

#### Bulk

Bulk determines how tough and sturdy a character is in combat. You gain:

- Ø +2 HP for every point of Bulk
- Ø +1 Recovery for every two points of Bulk

#### Agility

Agility determines how fast and evasive a character is in combat. You gain:

- Ø +1 Dodge for every point of Agility
- Ø +1 Speed for every two points of Agility

#### Mind

Mind determines how much mental strain a character can handle, and how well they can remember their training in combat. You gain:

- Ø +1 Stress Cap for every point of Mind
- Ø +1 Memory for every two points of Mind

#### Magic

Magic determines how magically adept a character is in combat. You gain:

- Ø +1 Aetheric Defense (A-Def) for every point of Magic
- Ø +1 MP for every two points of Magic

*Example:* Shawn wants Dhalia to be a spellcaster with a variety of spells, so she assigns 1 ability point to Mind and 1 to Magic.

Delmar wants Dmitri to be as tough as possible, so he assigns 2 ability points to Bulk.

#### <span id="page-12-0"></span>TALENTS

At level 1, choose three rank 1 talents. See [p. 202](#page-201-0) for the full list of talents and their abilities.

While many of the abilities available to beacons are tied to their class (see below), **talents** are sets of abilities that can be acquired by any beacon and that don't need to be equipped. Once your beacon has learned a talent, they will always have access to any abilities unlocked by their ranks in that talent.

Each talent has **three ranks**, each with its own ability. When your beacon first gains a talent, they get access to it at rank 1. If you assign additional points to that talent, you can get that talent's rank 2 and eventually its rank 3 ability.

Beacons gain additional talents and can even change their talent choices when they level up [\(p. 14](#page-13-2)).

*Example:* Shawn wants to pick magical-sounding talents for Dhalia. She picks the *Archmage*, *Enchanter*, and *Leyline Walker* talents because they sound like they will assist with magic. She doesn't spend much time reviewing all the options because she knows she can change them later.

Delmar, on the other hand, carefully reviews all the talents before making his decision. He chooses the *Brawler* talent because it enhances unarmed combat and he wants Dmitri to fight with his fists. He also wants to protect his allies, so he picks the *Knight* talent. Finally, he wants Dmitri to be highly mobile, so he chooses the *Traceur* talent.

#### <span id="page-12-1"></span>ANCESTRY

Choose an ancestry. See [p. 68](#page-67-0) for the full list of ancestries.

Your beacon's **ancestry** describes their heritage or species. Ancestry determines a beacon's Size and gives access to a list of **ancestry traits**. Whenever your character goes on a quest, they can choose to equip one of their ancestry traits.

Ancestries can also be used as collections of common traits, rather than literal descriptions of how characters look or act. For example, the **avian** ancestry allows for flight, but an avian character doesn't have to be a literal bird-person; maybe they're a human that comes from a culture where jetpacks are common.

*Example:* The elves in Shawn's favorite stories are known for being magically gifted, so she plans on making Dhalia an elf. She reads the elf ancestry traits and likes the *Elven Accuracy* one. She doesn't need to pick a trait until Dhalia goes on a quest, but she knows she'll probably use Elven Accuracy.

#### <span id="page-12-2"></span>CLASS

At level 1, choose 1 rank in any class. See [p. 84](#page-83-0) for the full list of available classes.

Classes represent specialized approaches to combat, like a Thunderclaw's electrical fighting style or a Phoenix's burning spells. Classes unlock equipment and abilities that beacons can equip for quests.

Each class has three ranks, with each rank unlocking additional equipment and abilities.

As they level up, beacons gain ranks in multiple classes. The equipment, techniques, and jobs they unlock can be mixed and matched in a huge number of combinations.

#### <span id="page-12-5"></span>Job

Each class also provides a **job** unique to that class. Jobs provide the starting values for a beacon's attributes ([p.](#page-13-1)  [14](#page-13-1)) and their equipment slots [\(p. 16](#page-15-0)). A class's job is unlocked upon reaching **rank 1** in that class.

Every job also has a **limit break** – a powerful, scene-changing ability unique to that class. Your beacon can only perform one limit break per quest. If you use a limit break from one job and later change jobs within the same quest, you don't get to use the new job's limit break – you've already used your one limit break for that quest. Beacons regain the ability to use a limit break after a **Long Rest** ([p. 61\)](#page-60-0).

*Example:* Shawn picks the *Equinox* class for Dhalia, gaining the Equinox job and unlocking the rank 1 *Exobomb* and *Scar* spells. She likes that the class focuses on long-distance spell attacks, as well as the description mentioning sun aether, which connects to her background skill "Disciple of the Sun".

#### <span id="page-12-3"></span>BASIC EQUIPMENT AND TECHNIQUES

All beacons have access to a wide array of **weapons**, **support items**, **skills**, and **spells** that they can equip right from the very beginning. They aren't limited to equipping only abilities from their current job.

You can find this standard gear in **Chapter 9: Basic Equipment and Techniques** ([p. 216\)](#page-215-0). You don't need to choose any until you're ready to go on a quest, but you might want to look at them now to get a feel for what's available.

#### <span id="page-12-4"></span>APPEARANCE

The last step in creating your beacon is deciding their appearance – what they, their equipment, and their abilities look like. It's totally up to you – you can describe them any way you want, regardless of the art and flavor text in this book.

The only limitation is that your description can't grant mechanical benefits in combat play. You can't give your character see-through skin and argue they should always be invisible. Beyond that restriction, describe whatever you want: Give your beacon wings that let them fly around during narrative play. Let them move around the world in a wheelchair. Have them wield weapons made of solid ice. Describe a photon axe as a greatsword.

Describe the character *you* see yourself playing. The information in this book is just intended to provide inspiration – it isn't absolute truth. There are infinite worlds in which your story can take place. Anything is possible.

*Example:* Shawn finishes character creation by deciding that Dhalia is a pale elf with long dark hair and golden eyes. Her spells flash with bright light when used, their color and luminosity changing depending on her emotions. With that, she's ready to begin playing!

### <span id="page-13-0"></span>LEVELS

Each beacon has a **character level**, which represents their overall growth and strength.

Whenever you finish a quest, whether you were successful or not, your beacon gains another character level. This is called leveling up. The **maximum character level** for beacons is 12.

#### <span id="page-13-2"></span>LEVELING UP

Whenever your beacon gains a character level, they gain:

- Ø 1 rank in a new or existing class
- Ø 1 ability point
- Ø 1 background skill point
- Ø 1 talent rank or a new rank 1 talent

#### GRIT

Grit represents the combat experience that beacons gain when they increase their character level.

Starting at character level 2 and then at every second level after that (i.e., levels 4, 6, 8, etc.), beacons gain +1 Grit. Grit is a passive bonus that applies to several other attributes. Each point of Grit provides the following:

- Ø +1 HP
- Ø **+1 to all attack rolls**
- Ø +1 Save Target
- Ø +1 Memory

#### CHANGING CHOICES

Whenever your beacon gains a level, you may do one or both of the following:

- Ø Reassign any number of ranks from one talent to another.
- Ø Reassign any number of ranks from one class to another.

### <span id="page-13-1"></span>ATTRIBUTES

In addition to their talents, background skills, and so on, beacons have several **attributes** that are used in combat play. They are determined by a character's level, job, ancestry, talents, ability scores, and equipped gear and techniques.

#### SIZE

Size determines how many spaces a character (or object) takes up on a grid and how they interact with cover. **Chapter 5: Combat Play** ([p. 41](#page-40-0)) covers how this works in greater detail. In an abstract sense, Size represents how much space or control a character exerts on the battlefield; it does not indicate exactly how tall or how heavy they are.

Size 1/2 and Size 1 characters both occupy 1 space, Size 2 characters occupy an area of 2 x 2 spaces, and Size 3 characters occupy an area of 3 x 3 spaces.

Beacons can be anywhere between Size 1/2 and Size 3. This is determined by their ancestry.

#### SCOPE

Scope is the maximum number of grid spaces within which a character can detect Hidden characters, attack with spells, and use certain other abilities.

#### <span id="page-14-0"></span>ARMOR

Armor reduces incoming damage by an amount equal to its value. Armor does not affect Discord, which is a nonstandard damage type, or Stress, which is not considered damage.

Characters cannot have more than 4 Armor.

#### SAVE TARGET

When one character forces another to make a save, the character making the save must equal or exceed the initiating character's Save Target to succeed.

#### HP AND WOUNDS

Every character has a pool of **hit points** (HP). Points are subtracted from this pool when they take damage.

Characters have a Bloodied **value**, which is half of their maximum HP (rounded up). When a character's current HP is equal to or less than their Bloodied value, they are considered Bloodied [\(p. 63](#page-62-0)).

When a character reaches 0 HP, they take a **Wound**. When they take a Wound, their player resets their HP, carrying over any excess damage, and makes a **wound roll** to see what additional penalties they suffer. Beacons are taken out of a fight when they accumulate **4 Wounds**. This is known as their Wound Cap.

See **Chapter 5: Combat Play** ([p. 41\)](#page-40-0) for additional information on HP, and **Wounds and Overstress** ([p.](#page-59-0)  [60\)](#page-59-0) for more information about Wounds.

#### RECOVERIES

Each beacon has a pool of Recoveries, which represent the resources they have available to heal themselves and repair their equipment. They can heal themselves by spending Recoveries using the Refresh action and other abilities.

Beacons start with a maximum number of Recoveries determined by their job, which increases with Bulk. They refresh their pool of recoveries when they take a **Long Rest** ([p. 61\)](#page-60-0).

#### DODGE

Dodge represents how hard it is for attacks to hit a character. To hit a target, the result of the attack roll must equal or exceed the target's Dodge.

#### SPEED

Speed is how fast a character can move. When a character takes a **standard movement** during their turn, they can move a number of spaces equal to their Speed ([p. 54](#page-53-0)).

#### STRESS CAP AND OVERSTRESS

**Stress** is a special type of harm that characters can take [\(p. 58\)](#page-57-0). Stress is accumulated through mental and physical strain, like suffering psychic attacks or performing tiring combat maneuvers.

When a character takes Stress, it accumulates until it exceeds their Stress Cap. If a character goes over their Stress Cap, they take an **Overstress.** Their player resets their Stress, carrying over any excess Stress; and make an **overstress roll** to see what additional penalties they suffer. Beacons are taken out of a fight when they accumulate **4 Overstresses**, which is known as their Overstress Cap.

Characters have a Breaking **point**, which is half of their Stress Cap (rounded up). When a character's current Stress is equal to or higher than their Breaking point, they are considered Breaking**.**

See **Wounds and Overstress** ([p. 60\)](#page-59-0) for additional information.

#### MEMORY

A character's Memory limits the maximum number of techniques they can equip. Each technique has its own Memory cost – the combined value of all of equipped techniques cannot exceed the character's Memory.

### AETHERIC DEFENSE (A-DEF)

Aetheric Defense (A-Def) is how hard a character is to hit with **Aetheric** attacks. **Aetheric** attacks target the aether that makes up a being, and they can't be avoided like normal attacks. These attacks – magical attacks from abilities with the **Aetheric** tag – must equal or exceed the target's A-Def to hit.

### MANA POINTS (MP)

**Mana points (MP)** are a pool of magical energy that can be spent to use certain abilities. To use one of these abilities, a character must have the indicated amount of MP available, and it is spent once the ability is activated. Beacons can restore MP in combat with the Refresh action.

### ABILITIES

Beacons use a wide variety of **abilities** to achieve victory in combat scenes. Abilities include traits, equipment, talents, and techniques. In general, anything that can be used in a combat scene is considered an ability.

#### Stacking Abilities

Each character can only benefit from one instance of any given ability or effect at a time. Put another way, subsequent instances of abilities and effects don't stack on top of themselves.

For example, the Sniper talent's rank 2 ability, Zero In, gives **+1** on ranged attacks every time you miss, and stacks until you finally hit with a ranged attack. Conversely, the Shocking Grasp spell, which prevents targets from performing reactions until the end of their next turn when they're hit, wouldn't extend its effect across additional turns if they were hit by Shocking Grasp again before the original effect expired.

#### TRAITS

Traits are abilities that reinforce a beacon's identity and playstyle. Traits come from a beacon's ancestry [\(p. 13](#page-12-1)) and equipped job [\(p. 13](#page-12-5)).

Traits can provide either passive benefits or effects that need to be activated. Some traits provide additional abilities, like the **Riftblade** job's special Riftcutter weapon. Beacons' equipped traits are always available and cannot be destroyed.

#### EQUIPMENT

Equipment is the weapons, armor, and tools that a beacon chooses to bring on a quest, and it has a significant impact on their combat performance. Equipment is broken down into two categories: **weapons** and **support items**. Equipment is usually selected when preparing for a quest.

#### <span id="page-15-0"></span>Equipment Slots

Each job provides a different number of **weapon slots** and **support slots**. These determine how many weapons and support items the beacon can equip. Weapons can only be equipped in weapon slots, and support items can only be equipped in support slots. Each slot is also suitable for equipment of a certain size – Light, Main, or Heavy – or smaller.

#### Equipment Size

Like equipment slots, weapons and support items come in three **sizes**, from smallest to largest: Light, Main, and Heavy.

You can only equip a weapon or support item into a slot if the equipment is the same size as the slot or smaller. For example, if you have a Main weapon slot, you can equip either a Main or a Light weapon to it.

#### Free Equipment

Some equipment can be equipped for free. Free equipment doesn't take up a slot when equipped; however, only one copy of each piece of equipment can be equipped for free at a time. You can't have multiple free versions of the same equipment. You can have multiple *different* pieces of equipment equipped for free, though, and something equipped for free can still be equipped in other slots.

#### Weapons

Weapons are the primary way that beacons attack their foes. Weapons can be either **melee weapons** or **ranged weapons**. Melee weapons make melee attacks, and ranged weapons make ranged attacks. See **Chapter 5: Combat Play** ([p. 41](#page-40-0)) for additional information on how different sorts of attacks work.

#### WEAPON TYPE

In addition to a size, each weapon has one of the following **types**:

- Ø Artifact**:** Wands, staves, and other magical implements. Artifacts often have the **Aetheric** tag.
- Ø Blade**:** Swords, axes, and daggers of any size. Blades are basic, balanced weapons.
- Ø Bow**:** Bows and crossbows. Bows are typically ranged and often have the **Arcing** tag, allowing them to fire over obstacles.
- Ø Club**:** Maces, warhammers, and other blunt weapons. These weapons are often **Slow** or **Inaccurate,**  but hit hard. Natural weapons like claws also count as clubs.
- Ø Longarm**:** Spears, javelins, and other weapons with an extended reach.
- Ø Magitech**:** Magitech weapons are any that combine technology and magic. Shardguns, which are firearms that shoot blasts of magical energy from specialized crystals, are the most common sort of magitech.

#### LINKED WEAPONS

Weapon slots that have a symbol between two weapon sizes (e.g., LIGHT LIGHT ) can hold two **linked**  weapons of the indicated sizes. These two weapons form a **linked pair**. When you attack with a linked weapon, you can also attack with the weapon it's linked to as part of the same action – as long as you haven't already done so as part of the same action. This second

attack is called a **linked attack**. Each linked pair can only trigger one linked attack per turn. You can make a linked attack even if the weapon would normally be used in a different phase. This excludes **Slow** attacks. Linked attacks don't benefit from bonus damage.

A weapon can only be in one **linked pair** at a time. Both weapons count as linked to each other. Each weapon in the linked pair counts as a separate weapon and can benefit from a separate **Enchantment**; however, if one is destroyed, so is the other.

#### WHAT DO LINKED WEAPONS LOOK LIKE?

Linked weapons can be interpreted a number of ways. They might be depicted as dual-wielded weapons; weapons literally connected by links; or weapons fused together, like a double-bladed sword.

#### Support Items

Support items are the armor, shields, potions, and tools that beacons can equip for quests. Support items often provide defensive bonuses or utility abilities.

#### TECHNIQUES

Beacons learn a wide variety of battle techniques that affect the way they approach combat. Offensive spells, unique ways of moving, and changes to one's own body are all types of techniques a beacon can equip for a quest. Techniques are broken down into two categories: **spells** and **skills.**

Techniques cost a certain amount of Memory to equip. A beacon's available Memory is determined by their equipped job, and increased by their Mind. You can only equip a technique if there is sufficient Memory available.

Techniques are usually equipped when preparing for a quest.

#### Free Techniques

Like equipment, some techniques can be equipped for free. Free techniques don't take up any Memory; however, only one copy of a given technique can be equipped for free at a time. You can't have multiple free versions of the same technique. You can have multiple *different* techniques equipped for free, though.

#### Spells

Spells are magical abilities. Not every spell requires the caster to spend MP, but they do require the caster to be able to manipulate aether. Because of this, the Silenced condition [\(p. 62\)](#page-61-0) prevents characters from casting spells.

#### Skills

Unlike spells, skills are abilities that don't require the user to manipulate aether. They are usually nonmagical in nature, but some do cost MP to activate, like the shapeshifter's Scout Form. These skills draw their mana directly from the user's body, with no active manipulation of aether needed. Because of this, skills are unaffected by the Silenced condition.

### RESOURCES

Beacons tend to acquire various resources during their adventures. Some resources are used for combat play only, like temp HP and Focus, while others are only used in narrative play, like gold and residuum. There are also **supplies**, that can provide benefits in both narrative and combat play.

#### COMBAT RESOURCES Temporary HP

Temporary HP (Temp HP) is a separate pool of HP outside of a character's normal HP. Whenever a character's HP is reduced by damage, points are taken from their Temp HP first. Any remaining damage is subtracted from their HP as normal. Temp HP can't be restored by effects that restore normal HP.

Temp HP can come from various sources, including techniques, equipment, and other abilities. Separate instances of Temp HP don't stack; if a character already has Temp HP, a second source of Temp HP would only apply if it is greater than the current amount of Temp HP they have. For example, if a character with 2 Temp HP uses an ability that would give them 4 Temp HP, this new, greater source of Temp HP replaces the first one – they end up with 4 temp HP, not 6.

#### Focus

Focus is a temporary resource gained during combat scenes. It can be spent for certain effects, like the Interpose **reaction**. It starts at 0, and has no maximum. All Focus is lost at the end of each scene.

### NARRATIVE RESOURCES

#### Gold

Instead of keeping track of every piece of currency your character is carrying, BEACON uses an abstract form of currency called Gold. Gold represents the resources and influence that are available to your beacon. The actual currency used in your Reflection might be emerald chips, paper bills, or credits, but for simplicity's sake these are all treated the same way.

Beacons are assumed to have enough resources to cover basic needs like food and housing, and they always have access to their starting and unlocked equipment. Gold is only used for downtime activities, purchasing supplies (see below), and in narrative scenes.

In narrative scenes, gold is only used for purchases that are meaningful and impactful. A beacon doesn't need to spend gold when staying at an inn or purchasing a drink at the tavern; however, staying in the penthouse suite of the Golden Goose Casino might cost gold, since doing so is a luxury reserved only for the wealthy.

Gold can be acquired through downtime activities like Pursue Profession or as a reward at the end of quests. Each beacon can have a maximum of 10 gold at a time.

#### WHY A GOLD LIMIT?

The Gold limit is to encourage players to spend their Gold more often, instead of hoarding it. GMs are welcome to change the limit to better suit their game.

#### <span id="page-17-0"></span>Residuum

Residuum is a byproduct of the destruction of magical items. It is dense in aether and can be used to modify other magical items. Residuum is created with the Disenchant Loot downtime activity. [\(p. 27](#page-26-0)).

- Ø 1 Residuum**:** Reroll any one loot crate slot.
- Ø 2 Residuum**:** Reroll any one loot crate slot and choose a specific type for the slot (weapon, support item, skill, or spell).
- Ø 2 Residuum**:** Reroll any one loot crate slot. The rerolled slot will be one level rarer than the original result (Uncommon > Rare > Legendary).

#### Supplies

**Supplies** are miscellaneous items that beacons can bring on quests, like a well-cooked meal from the tavern or a potion from the local alchemist. They provide temporary benefits and don't require equipment slots or Memory to equip. Supplies can be earned as quest rewards, through narrative play, most often with the Shop downtime activity. You can find the full list of supplies in **Appendix A: Shops and Supplies** ([p. 224](#page-223-0)).

When preparing for a quest, your beacon can equip up to three of their available supplies. Each beacon can only bring one instance of each supply per quest. All equipped supplies are destroyed at the end of the quest, regardless of whether they were used or not.

*Example:* Dhalia has two sleeping bags, a titan flask, and elf cookies as her available supplies. She is preparing for a tough quest, so she wants to bring as many supplies as she can. She can only bring 3 supplies and can't bring duplicates, so she brings one sleeping bag, one titan flask, and one elf cookie.

Supplies gained during the course of a quest can either be immediately equipped – exceeding the normal limit of three equipped supplies – or saved for a future quest.

# 3: QUESTS

Gameplay in BEACON revolves around quests. Quests are the adventures that the beacons go on, each comprising a combination of narrative and combat play.

After every quest, whether it is a success or failure, all beacons gain a character level.

Quests have the following components:

- Ø **Goal:** The quest's objective.
- Ø **Stakes:** The potential outcomes of success and failure.
- Ø **Information:** The things known by or available to the PCs before they begin the quest.
- Ø **Rewards:** The rewards, if any, that the beacons might receive upon completing the quest. Listed rewards might require succeeding in the quest, or simply completing it.

Quests progress through the following stages:

- Ø **Preparation:** The beacons prepare for the quest.
- Ø **Quest Scenes:** The quest begins and moves through a series of narrative and combat scenes that tell an overarching story.
- Ø **Downtime:** An opportunity to undertake downtime activities before the next quest.

**Chapter 10: Game Master's Guide** in the full version of BEACON provides guidance for creating quests.

### GOAL

The quest's goal is the thing the beacons need to do to succeed. This can include things like:

- Ø destroying the forces occupying the castle;
- Ø preventing the archmage from crashing the moon into the planet;
- Ø assassinating the leader of a faction;
- Ø rescuing a captured friend.

The players should always know the quest's goal.

### STAKES

The stakes describe the things that might happen if the quest succeeds or fails. Depending on the goal, this could include any of the following:

#### **Goal: Destroy the forces occupying the castle.**

- Ø **Success:** The nearby town is safe and its people don't need to worry about extortion.
- Ø **Failure:** The occupying force retaliates and destroys the town.

#### **Goal: Prevent the archmage from crashing the moon into the planet.**

- Ø **Success:** The moon stays where it is.
- Ø **Failure:** The moon crashes. Bad things follow.

#### **Goal: Assassinate the faction leader.**

- Ø **Success:** The faction is weakened and likely to be destroyed or assimilated.
- Ø **Failure:** The faction is emboldened, gaining more followers and strengthening its position.

#### **Goal: Rescue a captured friend.**

- Ø **Success:** The beacons' friend is returned safely.
- Ø **Failure:** The beacons' friend becomes the vessel of a parasitic psychic entity.

Each quest needs stakes, although the GM can keep them broad or vague. Either way, the players should have a good understanding of what the stakes are. The stakes can't be hidden or secret.

Depending on the actions of the players, it's possible to achieve a mixed outcome – neither an outright success nor a total failure. The GM can decide the results in cases like this. They don't need to be specified ahead of time.

### INFORMATION

Some information might be available to the players before the quest begins. This helps them prepare for the quest and understand the situation they're getting into.

Before the quest begins, the players should have an opportunity to ask questions, with the GM answering based on what they might reasonably know or can safely expect. The GM might also allow the players to perform skill checks or downtime activities to gain more information.

### REWARDS

After finishing a quest, the beacons receive rewards. Whether the quest was successful or not, beacons always receive a **new character level**, and a **loot crate**.

- Ø **New character level:** The beacons level up after every quest they complete. This increases their character level by one and allows them to gain a new class rank. They can either take a new class at rank 1 or go up to the next rank in a class they already have (up to a maximum of rank 3).
- Ø **Loot crate:** Loot crates are randomized rewards. Loot crates are an abstraction – a mechanism to assign random rewards to beacons – and they aren't always boxes of treasure. Each player receives their own loot crate containing three random pieces of equipment or techniques, one of which they get to keep. The loot they choose is added to their unlocked equipment and techniques. Players always have access to unlocked loot, just like any other piece of equipment or techniques. Each player can only receive a given piece of loot once; if a loot crate contains a piece of loot they already have, that option is rerolled. Loot crates are discussed in more detail in **Chapter 10: Game Master's Guide** in the full version of BEACON.

Beacons might receive additional rewards after a request, like gold, supplies, or narrative benefits. These additional quest rewards might be dependent on whether the quest was successful or not. For example, the rescue of a captured prince might see the beacons awarded noble titles. Clearing out a dangerous dungeon, on the other hand, might earn each player a specific bonus piece of loot. These additional rewards are up to the GMs discretion.

### QUEST STAGES

Quests can be broken down into three stages: preparation, the quest itself, and then downtime.

#### PREPARATION

When preparing for a quest, the players pick the abilities and equipment they want their beacon to have during that quest. They choose the following from their unlocked options:

- Ø A job
- Ø A trait from their chosen ancestry [\(p. 68](#page-67-1))
- Ø Equipment (weapons and support items) equal to their slots
- Ø Techniques (skills and spells) equal to their Memory
- Ø Supplies

Players always have access to all of their jobs, ancestry traits, equipment, techniques, and supplies when preparing for quests, regardless of where they are or the situation in which they find themselves.

Once everyone has prepared, the quest begins. From this moment on, they are considered to be on that quest until it is finished. They can only be on one quest at a time, although side objectives and challenges may arise along the way that weren't present when they set out.

### QUEST SCENES

Quests are composed of multiple narrative and combat scenes. Each quest typically features between one and four combat scenes, with narrative scenes sprinkled throughout.

The GM is responsible for presenting scenes to the players and stringing them together (see **Chapter 7: Game Master's Guide**, [p. 84](#page-83-0), for more information about building quests). While the GM might have an idea about how a scene could unfold, the actions of the players ultimately determine what happens within a scene.

The players are responsible for interacting with the scenes presented to them. Players don't get to choose what scenes take place (although they are encouraged to let the GM know if they have specific scenes they'd like to see), but they do influence what happens within a scene.

*Example:* The GM sets up a narrative scene where the beacons have to cross a heavily guarded bridge. They expect that the players will try to talk their way through; however, the players decide to solve the problem by building gliders and using magic to soar right over the bridge.

The GM did not expect this outcome, but the point of the scene was to see how the beacons solved the problem, not to force a social interaction. The players used an unexpected but creative solution.

#### Example Quest Structure

- Ø **Preparation:** The beacons are presented with a quest to find a specific villain and defeat them. They prepare for the quest and set out.
- Ø **Scene 1 (narrative scene):** The beacons scout for the villain's lair and contend with native fauna.
- Ø **Scene 2 (narrative scene):** The beacons find the lair, but must navigate past the traps and guards within.
- Ø **Scene 3 (combat scene):** The beacons confront the villain in battle.
- Ø **Scene 4 (narrative scene):** The beacons flee the villain's collapsing lair.
- Ø **Downtime:** The beacons return to town, completing the quest and receiving their rewards. They enter a period of downtime until they begin their next quest.

#### DOWNTIME

After finishing a quest, the beacons begin a period of **downtime**. Downtime is a type of narrative play in which the characters can pursue personal interests, explore the world, and perform **downtime activities** ([p. 27](#page-26-1)). The GM can also decide to declare downtime during long breaks in the middle of quests.

When the beacons begin downtime, it counts as taking a **Long Rest**, which replenishes their HP, MP, and other attributes; allows them to change out their equipment and techniques; and more. See [p. 61](#page-60-1) for more information on resting.

**Chapter 4: Narrative Play** contains further information on downtime (Downtime Activities, [p. 27](#page-26-1)).

# <span id="page-21-0"></span>4: NARRATIVE PLAY

Narrative play is anything that occurs outside of a combat scene, whether it be planning quests, pursuing personal objectives, disabling the traps in a ruin, or even running a tavern.

The players decide what their characters do during narrative play. If the GM believes an action is important, they can ask for a **skill check** to determine how well it goes.

Narrative play can involve small-scale fights and conflict, like fending off back alley robbers or overwhelming a single guard. These fights are resolved in an abstract fashion, using skill checks or **narrative actions** (see below) rather than tactical combat. Not every fight triggers a combat scene; combat play is reserved for important, large-scale fight scenes, like fending off a squadron of dragon riders from the deck of an airship during a thunderstorm.

NPCs don't use these narrative play rules. They don't have background skills or titles, and they don't roll dice or perform narrative actions. Instead, the GM describes what NPCs do or how they react to the things the PCs do. For example, if an NPC guard wants to capture a fleeing beacon, the guard wouldn't make a skill check. Instead, the GM would describe what the guard is trying to do and let the player describe how they attempt to avoid capture. If the beacon was in a bad situation or it was clear there would be no escape, they could instead just state that the beacon was captured. In either case, the GM makes no rolls.

### SKILL CHECKS

**Skill check summary:** When you make a skill check, you roll 1d20 with the following modifiers:

- **• +** equal to your ranks in a relevant background skill
- **• +2** if you invoke your beacon's title.

When you attempt something in narrative play that has a chance of failure, the GM may call on you make a skill check to determine whether you are successful or not.

To make a skill check, you **roll 1d20**. If you have a **background skill** that would be beneficial, you gain on the roll equal to your ranks in that background skill. Any given skill check can only benefit from one background skill. If you invoke your **title**, you gain **+2** to the roll (see [p. 11](#page-10-0) for information on titles).

Skill checks have three potential outcomes, depending on the total of your roll and any modifiers:

- Ø **Miss** (9 or less)
- Ø **Hit** (10–19)
- Ø **Crit** (20+)

A **miss** indicates you didn't achieve the desired result. You get a lesser version of what you were hoping to achieve and there's usually a significant cost you have to pay.

*Example:* Dhalia wants to research whether the Scourge's latest manifestation has a weakness. The GM asks Shawn to make a skill check to find out how Dhalia's research goes. She gets a 6 on her roll, which is a miss. The GM decides that instead of finding out a weakness, she learns of an ancient enemy of the Scourge. The GM also decides that she loses track of time and misses an important meeting.

A **hit** indicates success, but with some sort of cost. The cost isn't as severe as it would be on a miss, but it is still present.

*Example:* If Dhalia's result had been a hit, the GM would tell her that she successfully found one of the Scourge's weaknesses, but lost track of time and was late to an important meeting.

A **crit** is a resounding success. You get what you wanted at no additional cost, or you get even more than you were hoping for at a cost.

*Example:* If Dhalia's result had been a crit, the GM would just tell her that she found the Scourge's weakness, with no cost.

Costs for skill checks can include injuries, taking longer than expected, losing an item, damaging a relationship, embarrassing oneself, feeling a strong emotion, or anything else the GM or players can think of.

Skill checks can only be attempted at the GM's discretion. For example, if you try to jump 30 feet straight up in the air with nothing to aid you but an average level of human strength, the GM will disallow that skill check, even if you've already rolled a 25 on your attempt. Tell the GM what you want to do, and they will let you know whether you need to roll a skill check or not.

### DIFFICULT SKILL CHECKS

The sorts of activities that prompt skill checks are inherently difficult, since if they weren't, you shouldn't be rolling dice for them in the first place. That said, the GM can add to skill checks to represent particularly tough challenges.

If the GM or players believe a challenge is exceptionally hard, difficult, or challenging, the GM can impose up to **3** on the skill check.

- Ø **1** represents a very hard challenge.
- Ø **2** represents a challenge in which numerous factors all contribute to an exceptional level of difficulty.
- Ø **3** represents a challenge that straddles the line of what should even be allowed as a skill check. Before applying this level of difficulty, think about whether this is something that should be possible for a beacon to accomplish. If it is, then go for it – even if it stretches the limits of plausibility. After all, beacons are heroes that routinely do the impossible.

As a guideline, the GM usually shouldn't add more to a skill check than the tier of NPCs the beacons are currently up against.

#### HELPING OTHERS

Beacons can assist other beacons with their skill checks. To do so, the beacon who is helping must be in the same location as the one attempting the skill check and must be able to invoke their title, and the beacon making the skill check can't have invoked their own title for that check. If these criteria are met, the assisting beacon can invoke their title on behalf of the roller.

#### CHOOSING FAILURE

You can choose to automatically get a **miss** result on a skill check. You might do so if you find that result interesting or have a narrative reason.

### NARRATIVE ACTIONS

Skill checks work for anything a beacon might want to do in narrative play, but they are intentionally broad and generic. **Narrative actions** are specific types of skill checks that can also be made during narrative play and that have specific outcomes. They don't represent every possible action; instead, they are more detailed and focused variations of normal skill checks.

Narrative actions are used instead of standard skill checks when their **trigger** is met. Triggers are given as a **bolded phrase** in the action's description. This phrase is the event that takes place in the narrative to trigger the action.

When a narrative action is triggered, the player in question rolls a skill check and uses the results listed for that narrative action.

#### TERMINOLOGY

The terms **miss** and **hit** might sound familiar to you. They are borrowed from Meguey and Vincent Baker's Powered by the Apocalypse (PbtA) framework, first used in *Apocalypse World*. BEACON's narrative actions are designed like PbtA's "moves", meaning you can easily import other PbtA moves into BEACON, or even use an entirely different set of PbtA rules for narrative play.

#### CONVINCE

*A honeyed word, a veiled threat, or an equal exchange; all are ways of convincing a person to do something.*

**When you try to convince someone to do what you want, roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** They can't be convinced to do it.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The GM chooses one of the following:
  - They do what you want, but only if you do something for them first.
  - They won't do it unless you give up any leverage over them.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** They do what you want.

People can't be persuaded to do things that go against their beliefs or that they think are bad ideas unless you have some kind of leverage.

#### GAIN INSIGHT

*What you see is not always what you get. Sometimes the situation is unclear or confusing. Take a moment to look around and dive a little deeper.*

#### **When you quickly read a person, place, or situation, roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You get a bad read of things. The GM tells you either something untrue that you believe, or something true that you wish wasn't.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** Ask two of the following questions about your subject of study:
  - What recently happened or is about to happen?
  - How can I \_\_\_\_\_\_?
  - What seems strange here?
  - Who or what is in charge here?
  - What is safe or vulnerable here?
  - What is useful to me?
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** As above, and you can ask one follow-up question that doesn't need to be from the list.

#### GATHER INFORMATION

*Hit the streets or hit the books. Information is power, and you collect it from wherever you can find it.*

#### **When you spend time gaining information about a person, place, or thing, name your general topic of inquiry and roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** Ask the GM one question about your topic, and they will give you an answer that you think is true. The information is either untrue, or contains true information that you wish wasn't true.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** Ask the GM one question about your topic, and they will respond truthfully.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** As above, but you can ask two questions.

The GM can ask you to come up with a different question if they think it unlikely you would be able to discover that information, or if they want that information to be discovered another way. A classic example would be a beacon trying to solve a murder mystery: Asking, "Who is the murderer" might invalidate the quest, dampening the fun for everyone. On the other hand, the GM might allow the question, pivoting the quest from an investigation into a survival quest in which the PCs must outlast or escape the murderer!

#### MAKE CONFLICT

*Punch someone in the face, belittle them in front of their friends, or have a psychic showdown in their dreams. However you do it, you're in conflict with someone.*

This action is for quickly handling conflict in narrative play. Not every fight is important enough to warrant moving to combat play. The benefits of this move are purposely vague to encourage the players and GM to interpret the results creatively.

#### **When you engage in conflict with something (physical, social, or mental), roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** Choose two costs (see below).
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** Choose one benefit (see below) and one cost.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** Choose one:
  - You defeat them outright.
  - Choose two benefits and one cost.
  - Choose one benefit and no costs.

#### BENEFITS

- Ø You wound them or drive them away.
- Ø You leave an impression of your choice on them.
- Ø You create an opportunity for your allies.
- Ø You take something from them.

#### COSTS

*These costs have both narrative consequences as well as associated mechanical costs (in parentheses). The GM can decide whether to apply the mechanical cost or leave it as purely narrative.*

- Ø You are wounded (reduce all of your background skills by one rank until you have a chance to rest)
- Ø You put yourself or an ally in a bad position of the GM's choice (a character chosen by the GM gains +2 on their next skill check this scene).
- Ø You lose an important piece of equipment (a weapon or support item is destroyed).
- Ø You are shaken (a technique is destroyed).

#### MOVE UNSEEN

*Getting into a place you shouldn't be is easy for a beacon. The hard part is not getting caught.*

**When you sneakily gain access to an area, describe your plan and roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You find yourself in a bad position.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You get in, but getting out is harder. Choose one:
  - Ø You leave something behind.
  - Ø You take a problem with you.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You get in and out without anyone noticing.

Leaving something behind can include leaving an important item or evidence of your presence. Taking a problem with you can include taking injuries, alerting the guards, or taking a prisoner.

This action abstracts a variety of story beats and actions into one. Use it when you want to quickly handle a stealth scene.

### OVERCOME

*When an obstacle is in your way, go through it or around it. Jumping over a crocodile-filled moat or disabling a sentry-bot's control mechanism are all ways of overcoming an obstacle.*

**When you try to overcome an obstacle, state what you want to accomplish. The GM will tell you the stakes (the cost you must pay to succeed). If you accept, roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You don't succeed and must pay the cost.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You can choose to either succeed and pay the cost, or fail but don't pay the cost.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You succeed and don't pay the cost.

The stakes are a way for the GM to indicate how difficult a specific course of action is and telegraph this to the player. This also gives the player the opportunity to change their mind and think of a different approach.

For example, if the aforementioned crocodile-filled moat is particularly large, the GM could make the cost "severe damage to one of your limbs". The player might decide this cost is too high to pay, so they decide to think of another solution.

In the sentry-bot example, the GM could make the cost "your tools are destroyed". If the player decides they are comfortable with that cost, they can proceed to make the roll.

#### RECALL LORE

*Consult your accumulated knowledge to pull out random bits of lore and trivia, like what type of creature stands before you or the name of a specific noble.*

**When you recall your knowledge or memories about a topic, ask the GM a question and roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** The GM gives an answer you wish wasn't true, or you wish you didn't believe. They may ask what you think the answer is before answering.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The GM gives you a truthful answer. You can propose additional detail, but you can't invalidate the GM's answer.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** The GM gives an answer, and you can change or modify that answer as long as it remains in the spirit of the GM's original answer. The GM may ask what you think the answer is before providing theirs.

#### USE MAGIC

*Magic is powerful, but takes training to use safely. One small mistake can magnify a spell's undesirable effects.*

**When you attempt to create a magical effect, answer the following and roll:**

- Ø **Purpose:** What should the spell do?
- Ø **Boon:** Pick a positive trait for the spell (fast, quiet, powerful, etc).
- Ø **Bane:** Pick a negative trait for the spell (explosive, draining, loud, etc).

*The boon and bane shouldn't be opposites of each other.*

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** The spell fulfills its purpose, but gains the bane with no boon.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The spell fulfills its purpose. Choose one of the following:
  - Ø The spell gains its boon and its bane.
  - Ø The spell avoids its bane.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** The spell fulfills its purpose and gains its boon, but no bane.

### <span id="page-26-1"></span>DOWNTIME ACTIVITIES

Whenever your beacon has free time, they can perform **downtime activities**. There is automatically a period of downtime after the completion of a quest, but the GM can also declare downtime during a long break within a quest. A period of downtime allows each beacon to perform one downtime activity, unless the GM allows for more.

There is no specific length of time needed to perform a downtime activity, but at least a day is a good guideline. Some downtime activities are dependent on resources or other people and might not be available even if enough time exists to perform the activity.

Each downtime activity has a **bolded phrase** in its description, which is its **trigger**. When a beacon meets the trigger for a downtime activity, they can perform that activity. Some downtime activities involve rolls, while others have automatic effects.

If a player is unsure of which downtime activity to pursue, they can explain what they want to do. The GM and other players can then try to match that beacon's activity with an existing downtime activity or create a custom activity on the fly.

### CREATE A RITUAL

*Rituals are complex magical spells that can be customized to create whatever effects the caster wants. Due to their complicated nature, however, they are not easy to perform and can have dangerous side effects if done incorrectly.*

#### **When you want to use magic to solve a problem, answer the following questions and roll:**

- Ø What does it cost to cast the ritual?
- Ø How is the ritual performed?
- Ø What could go wrong?
- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** There's a problem with the ritual, and that problem is worse than you thought. When you use the ritual, the GM describes what goes wrong.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The ritual is more complicated than you expected. You and the GM each choose one of the following complications:
  - Ø It takes longer to create the ritual than you thought. You can automatically finish it by performing this activity again in a subsequent downtime period (no roll required) and meeting all of the other conditions.
  - Ø It goes wrong in the way you expected.
  - Ø The cost isn't sufficient. The GM adds an additional cost that must be paid to perform the ritual.
  - Ø The effects are unreliable or temporary.
  - Ø The ritual is considered forbidden or taboo.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You successfully create the ritual with no complications.

Once you've created a ritual, you can use it again without rolling if you meet the requirements and perform this downtime activity. Record your answers and the complications as the "recipe" for the ritual.

### <span id="page-26-0"></span>DISENCHANT LOOT

*Loot can be disenchanted into* residuum*, a magical powder used to enhance and alter other magical items ([p. 18\)](#page-17-0).*

**When you want to disenchant loot into residuum, choose at least one piece of loot.**

The loot you select is destroyed and you gain an amount of residuum depending on the rarity of the loot:

- Ø **Uncommon:** 3 Residuum
- Ø **Rare:** 4 Residuum
- Ø **Legendary:** 5 Residuum

#### DUNGEON-DIVE

*Dungeons and ruins are great places to find interesting gear and supplies.*

#### **When you want to acquire loot, roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** It was a rough expedition. You get a loot crate, but only the Slot 1 option.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The expedition was a success. You get a loot crate, but only the Slot 1 and Slot 2 options.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** Jackpot! You get a loot crate.

You can immediately sell or disenchant loot you get from this activity, as with the Shop and Disenchant Loot downtime activities.

#### <span id="page-27-0"></span>FIND ECHO

*The Source is made of countless reflections, each a different reality. Because of this limitless potential, it's possible to find alternate versions of yourself in the Source, called echoes. The strongest echoes are those that are almost exact copies of yourself, with only minor differences.*

Finding an echo requires meditation, slowly aligning your aether and mind with the Source. Once aligned, you receive a vision of the Source and all your echoes within. State the changes you wish to make to yourself, and the echoes will fade until only one remains. This echo has the aspects you want, and is willing to swap them with you for the versions you currently have.

**When you want to magically change your appearance, name, gender, title, ancestry, classes, or talents, find an echo.**

Once you find an echo, the changes take place. This is always a willing exchange, as the process can only take place with a willing echo. One thing that can't change are your life experiences, which remain the same.

This activity can be used to fully retrain your character, or to change your character's appearance to better match your image of them. It is not intended for shenanigans like getting away with crimes by changing your appearance, and characters trying to do so will discover that they can't find any suitable echoes.

Remember, you can make some changes to your character when they level up. Specifically, you can reassign any number of ranks in one talent to another and reassign any number of ranks in a class to another class. This activity is for bigger, more encompassing changes.

#### GAIN A CONTACT

*Contacts are relationships that can be leveraged for benefits. They can be people with any sort of relationship to a beacon, from close friends to rivals or even mortal enemies. Whatever the relationship, a contact can get you something you want.*

**When you want to find a person that can help you, name either a specific individual or a general category of person (dockworker, business executive, town guard, etc.), then roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You gain the contact, but you will need to earn their trust or deepen your relationship with them before they will do anything for you.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You gain this contact and they will perform a minor or moderate favor for you, but they will likely ask for something in return after doing so.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You gain this contact and they will perform a minor or moderate favor for you at no cost, or a major favor for which they will expect a favor in return.

Once the roll is made, the GM should set up a brief narrative scene to roleplay how they became your contact. The GM can disallow certain characters from becoming contacts, and should indicate this before a roll is made to gain them as a contact. GMs, don't make players use the downtime activity if it is impossible succeed.

#### GET SOMETHING

*Beacons can easily acquire common or mundane items like food, armor, and weaponry. Some things are harder to obtain – just out of a beacon's reach, but not impossible to grasp.*

#### **When you want to acquire something important, name what it is and roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You can get it, but at great cost. You either have to give up something you don't want to lose, or do something you don't want to do for someone.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You can get it, but you will owe something to someone.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You can get it, no strings attached. Next time you try to Get Something the same way, treat this result as a 10–19 instead.

Before you commit to performing this activity, the GM will let you know if the thing you want can't be obtained this way.

### GO SHOPPING

This downtime activity allows beacons to buy valuable items and supplies, or sell valuable items and loot for gold. When using it, keep in mind that beacons are always able to cover their basic needs, like food, housing, and their starting and unlocked equipment.

There are two versions of this downtime activity: **Buying** and **Selling**.

#### BUYING

**When you want to buy supplies or other valuable items, ask the GM for the available shops and their stock.**

Buying supplies or valuable items costs gold. You can buy as many items as you want from the shops available. The GM determines which shops are available, what they have in stock, and their prices, either manually picking options or randomly generating them (see [p.](#page-223-0) [224\)](#page-223-0). The goods in stock are the same for all beacons for the rest of the downtime period. The **general store** is always available.

A full list of shops and their supplies can be found in **Appendix A: Shops and Supplies** ([p. 224](#page-223-0)).

#### SELLING

**When you want to sell loot or other valuable items, name the items or loot and roll for each item:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You can only sell it for –1 gold below its base price at this time. You can try again with a future downtime activity.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You can sell it for its base price.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You can sell it for +1 gold above its base price.

The GM will set a base price of 1–3 gold. For loot, the rarity of the loot determines its price (**Uncommon:** 2 gold; **Rare:** 4 gold; **Legendary:** 6 gold).

#### WISHLISTING

When one beacon performs the Shop downtime activity, other beacons can also do so for free. Performing this activity for free is called Wishlisting. When Wishlisting, a beacon can only buy or sell one item.

| SHOPS                      | SUPPLIES                                   |
|----------------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| GENERAL STORE              | Adventuring supplies (p. 224)              |
| ALCHEMY SHOP               | Potions and elixirs (p. 225)               |
| BLACKSMITH                 | Gear and equipment (p. 227)                |
| ENGINEER                   | Magitech and cutting-edge tech<br>(p. 230) |
| TAVERN                     | Food and drink (p. 232)                    |
| LEATHERWORKER/<br>CLOTHIER | Gear and clothing (p. 228)                 |
| MAGIC SHOP/DOJO            | Temporary techniques (p. 233)              |

### HIT THE BOOKS

*Libraries, schools, and other repositories of information are valuable tools for beacons.*

**When you want to research or learn about something, name what you want to learn and roll:**

- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** You can ask the GM up to two questions about your topic before they answer. One of their answers can be untrue.
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** You can ask the GM up to three questions about your topic before they answer. One of their answers can be untrue.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You can ask the GM up to two questions about your topic, and both answers will be true. You don't need to ask both questions at the same time.

### IMPRINT A CRYSTAL

*Beacons can imprint memories of themselves onto special gems called job crystals. By focusing on a memory and weaving their aetheric field into the crystal, an imprint of that memory is made. Beacons holding a job crystal containing one of their imprints can access that memory by reweaving their aetheric field into it, overwriting certain features of themselves with the ones from the imprinted memory.*

**When you want to imprint a specific loadout onto a job crystal, pick any number of the following elements to imprint:**

- Ø An ancestry trait
- Ø A job
- Ø Weapons
- Ø Support items
- Ø Techniques
- Ø Clothing and general appearance

In **narrative play**, when you have a few moments to focus, you can replace your clothing, equipment, and gear with those imprinted on the crystal.

During a **Short Rest** or a **Long Rest**, you can replace your loadout with the imprinted loadout without spending a Recovery. The following caveats apply:

- Ø Your current HP, MP, Stress, and Recoveries don't change unless the new maximums are lower than your current values, at which point they are reduced to equal the new maximum.
- Ø If any equipment or techniques have been destroyed, their loss carries over to the new loadout:
  - Ø For every destroyed piece of equipment, a piece of the new loadout's equipment must be destroyed. The destroyed equipment must be of the same size, if possible. If there is nothing of matching size, something of the next largest size is destroyed instead. For example, if one of your Light weapons has been destroyed, one of the new loadout's Light weapons must also be destroyed. If the new loadout doesn't have any Light weapons, then you have to destroy a Main or Heavy weapon instead.
  - Ø For every destroyed technique, a number of the new loadout's techniques must be destroyed with a Memory cost equal or greater than the Memory cost of the destroyed techniques from the old loadout.

Equipment and techniques with the **Consumable** or **Limited** tags and no charges left count as destroyed when using an imprint.

A beacon can have one imprinted crystal at a time, and crystals can be used multiple times. To be used, the loadout imprinted on a crystal *must* have at least one difference from your current loadout. If an imprint matches the current loadout, it can't be used.

#### MAKE SOMETHING

*Beacons are capable of creating incredible items, whether they be suits that can survive the crushing depths of the ocean or airship engines that can traverse a storm wall.*

**When you want to make something special, answer the following questions and roll:**

- Ø What does it do?
- Ø Why is it special?
- Ø What is it made of?
- Ø **9 or less (Miss):** Something goes wrong in the design or crafting process. The GM picks two complications (see below).
- Ø **10–19 (Hit):** The object is more complicated than you expected. The GM picks one complication.
- Ø **20+ (Crit):** You make it with no complications.

#### COMPLICATIONS

- Ø It's expensive.
- Ø It requires a rare or unique ingredient.
- Ø It's unreliable or temporary.
- Ø It takes longer to make than you thought. You can automatically finish it by performing this activity again in a subsequent downtime period (no roll required) and meeting all of the other conditions.
- Ø You need help from \_\_\_\_\_.

Objects made using this activity are not usable in combat play. Once you've made an item, you can use this downtime activity to make the same item again without rolling as long as you have the ingredients. Record your answers as the "recipe" for the item.

This activity is for making special items. If a beacon wants to make something simple or uncomplicated, like carving a wooden duck, they can make it with a skill check at the GM discretion, instead of using a downtime activity.

#### PURSUE A PROFESSION

*Pursuing a profession can be a good way to earn* gold *or obtain* supplies*.*

Pursuing a profession has two steps, each of which is a downtime activity: **Learn a Profession** and **Work a Profession**.

#### LEARN A PROFESSION

**When you want to learn a new profession, pick one of the professions listed below, or create one and pick the type of business that you think would be connected to it from those listed below.**

You gain a new rank 1 background skill named for that profession. This background skill can be improved like a normal background skill.

You can have a maximum of two professions at a time, after which you can take this downtime activity to replace a known profession with a new one at rank 1. When you learn a profession, you can immediately **Work a Profession** using that profession.

| PROFESSION     | BUSINESS                               |
|----------------|----------------------------------------|
