19 Unfamiliar terrain

20 Unfriendly locals

*Example:* The beacons need to destroy a corrupted crystal deposit at the bottom of a mine. The crystal is spawning monsters and weakening the ground, so the GM declares an adversity challenge. They set the goal as "get to the bottom of the mine" and create three adversities: "monster infestation", "destroyed path", and "unfamiliar terrain". They decide this will be a simple challenge for four PCs, so they create two consequences: monster infestation will have the "hurt" consequence and the destroyed path will have the "delayed" consequence.

The GM describes the scene, explaining how the mine is old and full of dead ends (unfamiliar terrain) and damaged minecart rails (destroyed path), which are the quickest method of navigation. Monsters fill every corner of the mine, spawned from the crystal buried deep within (monster infestation).

Dhalia goes first. Because the mine is dark, she wants to illuminate it. The *Scout* approach is the best fit for her plan, so she makes a skill check with her "Disciple of the Sun" background skill. She gets a 12, which is a hit. She doesn't have a good way to overcome any of the consequences so she holds onto her choice until the next combat scene.

Barbazar goes next. She wants to use a minecart to get to the bottom of the mine quickly. Because the rails are damaged, her plan is to use her incredible strength to lift and jump the minecarts across the damaged sections. She picks the *Endure* approach and makes a skill check with her "Bend Bars and Lift Gates" background skill. She rolls a 14, also a hit. One of her weapons was destroyed in an earlier combat, so she picks the "Armorer" option to immediately repair it.

Mallory wants to get rid of the monsters by hacking and slashing at them while riding past them in the minecart. She picks the *Whittle* approach and makes a skill check with her "Murderous Ball of Death" background skill. She rolls a 23, which is a crit! She chooses the "Surpass" option, overcoming the monster infestation consequence and holding her other choice until later.

The GM decides that the actions of the beacons have gained the attention of a large monster in the mine. They describe a giant monster that starts chasing the PCs down the tunnels, adding a new adversity (but no new consequences).

Dmitri wants to hold the giant monster off until they can reach the bottom of the mine. He uses the *Endure* approach and his "Perfected Body" background skill to jump in the monster's mouth and keep it from biting anyone. He rolls a 17 and immediately chooses the "Defensive" option.

Now that every PC has acted, the GM describes how the PCs soar down to the bottom cave, the giant monster stuck in the entrance, too large to enter. The guardian of the crystal materializes and the beacons enter a combat scene. Everyone that didn't already choose a hit or crit effect from their approach can do so now. Because no one overcame the "destroyed path" consequence, all beacons will suffer the effects of the "hurt" consequence in this combat.

# 5: COMBAT PLAY

This chapter contains the rules governing combat scenes. Combat scenes are tactical, rules-heavy parts of the game and the main focus of BEACON. This chapter explains how combat scenes work, and the rules and terminology used in combat play.

By default, there are two sides in every combat scene: the PCs and the NPCs. The rules in this chapter apply to PCs and NPCs, but NPCs have slight differences that are explained in **Chapter 11: Non-Playable Characters** in the full version of BEACON.

### SUMMARY

Combat in BEACON is composed of a few different units of time: **scenes**, **rounds**, **phases**, and **turns**.

**Scenes:** A **scene** is an entire combat, from beginning to end. Once the fighting is over, the scene ends.

**Rounds:** Each combat scene is made up of a number of **rounds**, each of which is split up into eight distinct **phases**. At the end of the eighth phase, the round ends and another round begins.

**Turns:** Each character takes one **turn** per round, taken during one of that round's phases. The action they want to take on their turn determines which phase they act in. On their turn, a character can perform their **standard movement**, one **standard action**, one **minor action**, and any number of **free actions** (within reason). Most NPCs only take one turn per round, but some NPCs might take multiple turns.

The players and the GM's NPCs take turns on an alternating basis, with priority going to whichever side (PC or NPC) hasn't acted most recently.

The PCs generally take the first turn in the first round of a combat scene. When multiple PCs want to take a turn at the same time, the PCs decide their order, and if an agreement can't be made, the GM will decide.

*Example:* In the first round of combat, two PCs and one NPC want to act during Phase Skirmish. No characters have taken a turn yet, so one of the PCs gets to take the first turn. Once they've taken their turn, the NPC can take their turn, followed by the final PC.

Later that round, another PC wants to take their turn during Phase Full Attack. Normally an NPC could take their turn, but since there are no NPCs that can act during this phase, the PC takes their turn.

<span id="page-40-0"></span>**Phases:** Phases are the various stages of the round during which characters can act. The phase in which a character takes a turn is usually decided by the action they want to perform, as each action can only be performed in a specific phase. For example, characters can only Defend during Phase Bolster and can only Grapple in Phase Brawl.

At the beginning of each phase, the side whose turn it is to act can nominate a character to take a turn (as long as they haven't already taken one this round). Once they've taken their turn, the other side may nominate a character, and so on.

Once all characters that want to take a turn during a given phase have done so, the next phase begins.

#### THE BATTLEFIELD

Combat scenes in BEACON are played out on a battle map that uses either square or hex-shaped spaces (either is fine). The battlefield is **two-dimensional – it is a flat plane, with no vertical spaces;** however, there are several mechanisms to represent height advantages, like a character's Size and the Flying status ([p. 63\)](#page-62-1).

#### INFORMATION TRANSPARENCY

Information transparency is important in combat scenes. The players should always know:

- Ø The NPC types and templates for all enemies.
- Ø The locations of enemies on the map at all times, regardless of line of sight or whether they are Hidden or Invisible.
- Ø The conditions and statuses of all enemies.
- Ø The battle plan and all relevant goals.
- Ø The locations of all **Traps**.
- Ø The mechanics of any abilities or items that NPCs use.

Other information is restricted unless a beacon has a specific way to access it. This includes the ability scores and attributes of NPCs and the specific features they have equipped. Certain abilities, like the Lore Book support item [\(p. 218\)](#page-217-0), can provide access to restricted information.

#### ENDING THE SCENE

Combat scenes end when all enemies are defeated or when the combat's objective has been achieved.

At the end of a combat scene, all effects, conditions, statuses, and combat resources (Temp HP, Focus) go away unless explicitly stated.

### PHASES

Each round has the following phases:

| PHASE       | ACTIONS AVAILABLE |             |         |  |
|-------------|-------------------|-------------|---------|--|
| Bolster     | Defend            |             | Refresh |  |
| Channel     |                   | Channel     |         |  |
| Skirmish    | Mark              | Search      | Volley  |  |
| Reposition  | Rush              |             | Hide    |  |
| Brawl       | Fight             | Shove       | Grapple |  |
| Release     |                   | Release     |         |  |
| Full Attack |                   | Full Attack |         |  |
| Delay       |                   | Delay       |         |  |
| Any Phase   |                   | Ready       |         |  |

### TURNS

During their turn, each character can perform one **standard movement**, one **standard action**, one **minor action**, and any number of **free actions** (within reason) in any order.

**Standard movement:** A movement of a number of spaces up to a character's Speed.

**Standard action:** A basic action available during the current phase.

**Minor action:** A quick or simple action, often granted by an ability.

**Free action:** A special action granted by some abilities. Characters can take any number of free actions during their turn (within reason).

Certain abilities will indicate either **the phase in which they can be performed** or **an action that must be performed** to use them.

For example, a technique that can only be used in Phase Bolster would say "Phase Bolster". One that specifically requires the Defend action – a basic Phase Bolster action – would say " Defend".

If an ability lists an **action** (e.g., Volley), you use that action to activate the ability instead of the normal effects of the action. For example, the Heroic Leap skill requires the Rush action to activate. A beacon can use this skill when they take the Rush action, but can't also move up to their Speed per the normal Rush action effects ([p. 44](#page-43-0)). If an ability requires a **minor action** or **free action**, it can be performed during any phase as long as it is your turn.

If an ability lists a **phase** but not an action (e.g., Phase Skirmish) activating it requires you to perform it as your standard action during the indicated phase, but it doesn't count as one of that phase's basic actions (Volley, Defend, etc.) for the purposes of triggered effects. For example, the rank 1 Knight talent Phalanx does something when you Defend. Using the Aegis spell Barrier, which has an activation of Phase Bolster, would not trigger that effect, because you are not using the Defend action.

NPCs take their turns in a different way. Each NPC has an Initiative score, and they take their turn in whatever phase matches their Initiative. Unlike PCs, though, their actions aren't limited by the current phase. For example, the Ardent NPC type has Initiative 1. They always take their turn in Phase Bolster, and they could use their standard action in that phase for any action, not just Defend or Recover.

*Example:* Dhalia wants to attack with her bow, which is a Volley weapon. This requires her to use the Volley action during Phase Skirmish, because that phase allows the use of Volley. If she has any abilities that trigger off of the use of a Volley action, like the Pincer Strike loot skill, they also go into effect.

Later, Dhalia wants to use her Mock skill from the **Skald** class. This skill requires a Phase Skirmish action to use, so Dhalia can only use it during Phase Skirmish. Doing so doesn't count as using a Mark, Search, or Volley action.

#### SURGING

Each beacon can Surge once per round. Doing so allows you to take a second turn at the cost of taking Stress. Each time you Surge, it costs more Stress.

All beacons have a Surge level, which starts at 0 and determines how much Stress they take from using Surge (see below). After they Surge, they increase their Surge level by +1, to a maximum of 3. A beacon's Surge level resets to 0 when they take a Long Rest.

At the beginning of a Surge turn, before anything else happens, your beacon takes Stress based on their Surge level.

Surge turns count as normal turns for the purposes of effects that start or end in your next turn. When taking Surge turns, your beacon can perform their **standard movement**, **standard action**, **minor action**, and **free actions** as normal. You can't Surge before you've taken your first turn in a round.

| SURGE LEVEL | STRESS COST  |
|-------------|--------------|
| 0           | 2 Stress     |
| 1           | 1d3+1 Stress |
| 2           | 1d6+2 Stress |
| 3           | 1d6+4 Stress |

#### ALTERNATIVE SURGE RULES

Surge is a powerful tool that allows PCs to change the game's action economy. Because of this, some groups may find that it doesn't suit their game for various reasons, like the potential for increased turn length or the effect it can have on one-shot games.

Here are some alternative rules you can try if your group wants to rein in the power of the default Surge rules, although it is recommended that you try the normal rules first.

- Ø **Higher starting cost:** The beacons start at a higher Surge level. For example, they might start at Surge level 2 instead of 0. This simple change is often used for one-shot games.
- Ø **Partial Surge:** When beacons Surge, they get *either* a standard action, a standard movement, or a minor action, instead of all three. All other rules remain the same.
- Ø **Restricted Surge.** PCs can only Surge once per scene, with a flat cost of 1d6+1 Stress. The human ancestry's **Adrenaline** trait instead grants **resistance to Stress** from Surge, and the Rimeguard's Absolute Zero limit break clears their own Stress and that of all allies in the scene and grants the affected characters **resistance to all Stress** for the rest of the scene.

### ACTIONS

There are four types of actions characters can take: **standard actions**, **reactions**, **minor actions**, and **free actions**.

Some abilities grant characters additional options when they use an action. For example, a beacon with the Rank 3 **Brawler** talent Submission gains a unique Channel action. When they Channel, they can choose to use the effects of Submission instead of one of their other Channel abilities.

Certain conditions, like Stunned, limit the actions that a character can take.

### SIMULTANEOUS ACTIONS

When multiple actions or effects would affect a character at the same time, such as at the beginning of their turn, the following rules apply:

#### **Actions and effects from other characters apply first:**

- Ø Roll checks and saves first.
- Ø If there are multiple effects from one character, they choose the order in which they happen.
- Ø If there are effects from multiple characters, the GM decides the order.

#### **Self-imposed actions and effects apply second:**

- Ø Roll checks and saves first.
- Ø You determine the order.

*Example:* An enemy **Cleaver** is starting their turn adjacent to Dmitri, who is using the **Warden** job. Two effects are triggering at the start of the Cleaver's turn. The Cleaver's Whirlwind skill ends one of its benefits, and the Warden trait Grasping Vines allows Dmitri to apply a negative effect to the Cleaver. Dmitri's effect will apply first, and the Cleaver's selfimposed effect will apply second.

If Dhalia had an effect that was also triggering at the start of the Cleaver's turn, the GM would decide whether Dmitri's or Dhalia's effect would happen first.

#### STANDARD ACTIONS

This section lists the **standard actions** that you can perform on your turn, broken down by phase.

— Phase 1: Bolster —

#### — Phase 3: Skirmish —

**Mark** Phase Skirmish

#### **Defend** Phase Bolster

You gain Focus equal to your Armor+1d3. Until the start of your next turn, attacks against you are made with **+1** .

**Refresh** Phase Bolster

Choose one of the following:

- Ø Spend a Recovery to regain all HP.
- Ø Clear all Stress.
- Ø Regain all MP.

Then choose one of the following:

- Ø Reload all **Reloading** weapons/abilities.
- Ø Clear all Discord.
- Ø Clear a condition that wasn't caused by yourself.
- Ø Clear a condition on an adjacent ally that wasn't caused by them.

#### — Phase 2: Channel —

#### **Channel** Phase Channel

You begin to enact a Channel ability, the effect of which you can unleash in Phase with the Release action. This is the only way to trigger a channeled ability's Release effect. Until you release it, you are considered to be **channeling**.

While channeling, if you take any damage (after Armor) or **Stress** not caused by yourself, you must pass a Bulk save against the other character's Save Target. This is called a **concentration save**. On a failure, you lose **concentration**: you stop channeling and the ability fizzles.

You can only channel one ability at a time, and you can't Ready the Channel action. You can stop channeling as a **free action**. If you don't make a **standard movement** this turn, you can do so when you Release the ability this round.

Channel abilities are considered to be used at the time this action is taken, and any costs (e.g., MP) must be paid at that time. Their effects, including choosing targets, only happen when they're released unless the ability specifically states they happen when channeling.

A character within your Scope and line of sight becomes Marked.

#### **Search** Phase Skirmish

Choose a character or object within Scope and pick one:

- Ø If they are Hidden, you make a contested Mind check against their Agility. On a success, they are no longer Hidden.
- Ø You learn the target's ability scores, attributes, and abilities.

#### **Volley** Phase Skirmish

You make one of the following attacks:

- Ø **Ranged attack:** Make a ranged attack with an ability against a target within the ability's Range. Roll 1d20+Grit. If your result meets or exceeds the target's Dodge (or A-Def for **Aetheric** attacks), it hits.
- Ø **Improvised spell attack (**1 MP**)***:* Make an **Aetheric** ranged spell attack against a character within Scope. Roll 1d20+Grit. If your result meets or exceeds the target's A-Def, they take 2 Stress .

#### — Phase 4: Reposition —

#### **Hide** Phase Reposition

You become Hidden.

#### <span id="page-43-0"></span>**Rush** Phase Reposition

Choose one of the following:

- Ø You move up to your Speed.
- Ø Until the end of the turn, you ignore Engagement and don't provoke **reactions** from movement.

#### — Phase 5: Brawl —

#### **Fight** Phase Brawl

You make one of the following attacks:

- Ø **Melee attack:** Make a melee attack with an ability against a target within the ability's Reach. Roll 1d20+Grit. If your result equals or exceeds the target's Dodge (or A-Def for **Aetheric** attacks), it hits.
- Ø **Improvised weapon attack:** Make a melee weapon attack against an adjacent target. Roll 1d20+Grit. If your result equals or exceeds the target's Dodge, they take 1d3 Physical .

#### **Grapple** Phase Brawl

You make a **grapple attack:**

Ø **Grapple attack:** Make a melee weapon attack against an adjacent character. Roll 1d20+Grit. If your result equals or exceeds the target's Dodge, you and the target are both Grappled.

Grapple attacks can be made while you are Disarmed ([p. 62\)](#page-61-1).

### **Shove** Phase Brawl

You make a **shove attack:**

- Ø **Shove attack:** Make a melee weapon attack against an adjacent target. Roll 1d20+Grit. You gain **+1** for every Size you are larger than the target, or **+1** for every Size smaller. If your result equals or exceeds the target's Dodge, choose one of the following:
  - You **push** them **1 space**.
  - You knock them Prone.

Shove attacks can be made while you are Disarmed ([p. 62\)](#page-61-1).

#### — Phase 6: Release —

#### **Release** Phase Release

You release an ability you are channeling, causing it to take effect. This is resolved as if you were taking a turn, following the rules for alternating turns between PCs and NPCs, but doesn't count as a turn. Instead of releasing an effect, you can continue channeling it and release it during Phase in the next round. You can't release an ability if you are Silenced.

#### IMPROVISED ATTACKS

Volley and Fight allow characters to make **improvised attacks**. Improvised attacks are desperate attacks with whatever is nearby. An improvised weapon attack might use a nearby rock or even your bare hands, while an improvised spell attack might be an incredibly simple spell or a fragment of a more complex spell.

Improvised attacks allow characters to always have the ability to attack and deal damage or Stress, even if their equipped abilities get destroyed. Improvised attacks don't require any training to use, and are usually inferior to equipped abilities.

#### — Phase 7: Full Attack —

**Full Attack** Phase Full Attack

**1/round**, choose one of the following:

- Ø You use a **Slow** ability.
- Ø You perform any two of the following actions Volley, Fight, Shove, or Grapple. You can choose the same action twice, but not the same ability or the same linked pair of weapons.

#### WHY CAN'T I USE THE SAME ABILITY/ACTION TWICE?

To encourage variety. If you could use the same weapon multiple times, you might find yourself using your best weapon twice and only using secondary options when the primary one is destroyed. This way you need to use different options, and because of that, look for interesting synergies.

#### WHAT DOES ATTACKING WITH TWO DIFFERENT WEAPONS LOOK LIKE?

Rounds don't represent specific amounts of time. A round could be six seconds, minutes, or even days. This means you can describe it however you want. Maybe you wield one weapon in each hand. Maybe you swap them out. Maybe one gets stuck in your foe, and you draw another one. It's up to you, and doesn't matter mechanically.

#### — Phase 8: Delay —

**Delay** Phase Delay

The last chance to take an action during this round. If you wanted to take an action but the phase had already passed, you can do so in this phase.

#### — Any Phase —

**Ready** Any Phase

You prepare your standard action at a later moment, turning it into a customizable **reaction**:

- Ø Select a trigger, which must be a specific **action**, **movement**, or **reaction** taken by any character.
- Ø Select your effect, which must be an **action** or **minor action** you can take during your current turn.
- Ø Until the start of your next turn, you can perform that readied action as a **reaction** when the trigger is met. If you haven't used your readied action by the start of your next turn, the action is lost. You can't use any other **reactions** while you have an action readied. You can forgo your readied action at any time.

#### **Example Readied Action** Reaction

**Trigger:** Any enemy *moves* outside of cover.

**Effect:** I will Volley at them.

The Ready action is used to set up an action for later. It can be used during any phase, but the action you set up for later is limited by the phase you are in and it must be an action you can currently take. For example, if you are Disarmed, you can't ready a Volley action to attack with one of your ranged weapons, because the Disarmed condition is preventing you from making that weapon attack.

#### REACTIONS

In addition to actions, you can also perform **reactions**. Reactions are special actions that are performed in response to specific triggers, even on other characters' turns.

You can **perform one reaction per turn**, but there's no limit on how many times a specific reaction can be used unless one is specified (e.g., 1/round).

Some NPCs (typically elite enemies or mini-bosses) can take more than one turn in a round. For these NPCs, reaction limits like 1/round reset at the start of each of their turns *and* at the start of each new round.

Each reaction has a **trigger** and an **effect**. The **trigger** is the event or occurrence that needs to take place for the reaction's effect to occur. The **effect** is what happens when the trigger is met.

Unless otherwise noted, reactions resolve *after* their triggers. If a reaction's trigger is, "when attacked," the effect takes place after the attack has been resolved (including any damage). A reaction with a trigger of, "when targeted," or, "when an attack is declared but before the roll is made," on the other hand, goes into effect before the attack happens.

Some effects, like **forced movement** [\(p. 55](#page-54-0)), don't provoke reactions even if a reaction's trigger would be met.

These are the basic reactions available to all PCs. You can acquire additional reactions through equipped abilities.

# Focus 1

**Counterattack** Reaction (1/round)

**Trigger:** You are hit by an attack from a character within the Reach of one of your abilities.

**Effect:** You Volley or Fight against that character with one of those abilities. Each attack roll can only be counterattacked once – multiple characters can't Counterattack in response to the same attack roll.

#### **Interpose** Reaction Focus 1

**Trigger:** An ally within Range 3 is targeted by an attack.

**Effect:** You **shift** up to 2 spaces. If you end this movement next to the targeted ally, you can choose to become the target instead, even if you would otherwise not be a valid target. Each attack roll can only be interposed once – multiple characters can't Interpose in response to the same attack roll.

#### **Opportunity Attack** Reaction (1/round)

Reach of one of your abilities.

**Trigger:** An enemy begins a movement within the

**Effect:** You immediately Volley or Fight against that character with one of those abilities.

### MINOR ACTIONS

You can perform one **minor action** during your turn. Minor actions can be used during any phase.

You can acquire minor actions through equipped abilities.

#### FREE ACTIONS

**Free actions** can only be taken during your turn, but you can perform any number of free actions during that turn (within reason).

You can acquire free actions through equipped abilities.

### <span id="page-47-0"></span>CHARACTERS AND OBJECTS

In combat, everything on the battlefield can be separated into two categories: **characters** and **objects**.

#### CHARACTERS

PCs, **Summons**, and NPCs are all considered **characters**. As a general rule, characters are entities capable of taking some sort of actions, either personally or at the command of other characters.

A character's abilities are considered part of that character, not separate objects or characters (unless explicitly stated otherwise).

#### Allies and Enemies

There are two sides or teams in every combat scene. The PCs are on one side and the NPC enemies are the other.

Characters in a combat can be **allies** or **enemies** of the other characters. A character friendly to you or on the same side is an **ally**; a character hostile to you or on the other side is an **enemy**. The same applies in reverse.

**You don't count as your own ally.**

#### OBJECTS

An **object** is anything that isn't a character (or part of one). The terrain, parts of the environment, and deployed items (like **Traps**) are all objects.

Only things that fit this definition count as objects in the context of the rules. While we might describe a character's sword as an object in conversational language, it does not count as one in game terms because it is part of a character. You couldn't target or affect the sword with an ability that targets objects.

#### Terrain

**Terrain** is a specific subset of object that includes the environmental elements in a combat scene – trees, walls, rocks, and similar. **Deployed objects**, like **Traps**, are objects but don't count as terrain. Terrain is different from terrain features ([p. 57](#page-56-0)), which are effects that modify terrain.

The GM determines what counts as terrain and what doesn't.

#### Interactable Objects and Terrain

Some objects and terrain is interactive, meaning that characters can use actions on them for specific effects. **Treasure chests**, for example, require a **minor action** to open.

### <span id="page-48-0"></span>ABILITY CHECKS AND SAVES

In combat, players make **ability checks** and **saves** instead of **skill checks**.

**Ability check and save summary: Roll 1d20 + indicated ability score + or** .

Effects that prompt ability checks or saves indicate which ability score – Bulk, Agility, Mind, or Magic – is used for the roll. Background skills, Grit, and titles don't apply.

To succeed on an **ability check**, you need a result of 10+.

To succeed on a **save**, your result must equal or exceed the Save Target of the initiator, or 10 if not otherwise specified.

### TARGETING

Abilities can either affect their user or target something else on the battlefield. Targeted abilities, like attacks, can be affected by obstacles or the layout of the battlefield.

To target something with an ability or effect, the potential target must be:

- Ø within line of sight [\(p. 49\)](#page-48-1);
- Ø within Range or Reach ([p. 53\)](#page-52-0); and
- Ø either a character, a space on the battlefield, or an object ([p. 48\)](#page-47-0).

You can't attack yourself unless otherwise indicated.

### <span id="page-48-1"></span>LINE OF SIGHT

All abilities require a clear path to their target, known as **line of sight**, unless otherwise noted.

Line of sight refers to a clear line of effect to a target. Walls and other sufficiently large objects can block line of sight, while smaller objects like crates or shrubs might provide cover instead of fully blocking line of sight ([p.](#page-49-0) [50\)](#page-49-0). While BEACON doesn't represent or measure vertical space, the Size of characters and objects is used as a way to determine line of sight.

Line of sight is reciprocal. If you can draw line of sight to another character, then the second character also has line of sight to you.

Line of sight does not literally require sight. For example, a character with no eyes can still draw line of sight, and a transparent wall of force still blocks line of sight even though you can see through it.

Certain effects (like the **Arcing** and **Homing** tags) allow you to bypass the usual rules regarding line of sight. These tags not only allow you to draw line of sight in situations where you normally couldn't, but also allow for one-directional line of sight.

#### Determining Line of Sight

To determine line of sight, draw a straight line from the corner of one of the spaces you occupy to a corner of one of the spaces occupied by the target. If the line crosses a piece of **hard cover** that is a larger Size than **both** parties, then line of sight is blocked.

*Example:* You and your enemy are both Size 1, and are on opposite sides of a Size 3 wall. Neither of you can draw line of sight to the other; however, if you have a weapon with the **Arcing** tag, you could shoot over the wall and the enemy still wouldn't have line of sight to you.

**FIGURE 1:**

**Example A:** Line of sight can be drawn and

there is no cover.

**Example B:** Line of sight cannot be drawn.

**Example C:** Line of sight can be drawn, but

cover applies.

#### <span id="page-49-0"></span>COVER

**Objects on the battlefield can provide cover.**  Cover imposes on attacks and can block line of sight, depending on whether it is **hard cover** or **soft cover**.

Cover is most often an object, but it can also be a **zone** ([p. 53\)](#page-52-1) or effect. Some abilities can create pieces of cover on the battlefield. These created pieces of cover count as objects.

Pieces of cover have Sizes just like PCs. A Size 1 piece of cover fills 1 space, a Size 2 piece of cover fills a 2 × 2 space, and so on. Cover can sometimes cover more spaces than its Size would normally indicate; for example, a tall, thin column might only occupy 1 space despite being considered Size 3, or a continuous wall surrounding a castle might be Size 2, but occupy all the spaces around the castle.

You can only benefit from cover if it's the same Size as you or larger. For example, a Size 2 character does not gain the benefits of cover from a Size 1 piece of cover.

There are two types of cover: **soft cover** and **hard cover**. Soft cover and hard cover don't combine with each other. If you would gain the benefits of both soft and hard cover, you count as having hard cover.

#### Soft Cover

**Soft cover** grants **+1** to attacks, but doesn't block line of sight.

Soft cover is either cover that partly blocks a character, or isn't strong enough to fully shield them from attacks, like smoke or thin walls.

#### Hard Cover

**Hard cover** grants **+2** to attacks and can block line of sight.

Hard cover is cover that is large or strong enough to reliably block attacks, like thick walls.

To benefit from hard cover, you must be adjacent to the source of cover. Otherwise, it only provides soft cover. **Hard cover blocks line of sight.**

In **Figure 1: Example B**, the hard cover blocking line of sight means neither character has line of sight to the other. In Example C, they've positioned themselves so they have line of sight to each other, but cover applies. Because both characters are adjacent to the cover, they both benefit from hard cover. If one character was to move away from the cover, they would instead only benefit from soft cover, while their enemy retains hard cover for remaining adjacent to it.

#### Determining Cover

To determine if cover affects an attack, draw a line from the center of the space you occupy to the center of the target. If the line crosses any cover that is the same Size as the target or larger, your attack receives .

Abilities with the **Indirect** or **Homing** tags ignore the effects of cover. Abilities with the **Arcing** tag, on the other hand, don't have their line of sight blocked by cover, but are still affected by the cover provides.

#### **FIGURE 2:**

**Example A:** The soft cover does not break line

of sight, but both characters gain

cover.

**Example B:** The cover doesn't break line of sight

because it isn't larger than both characters. Neither character is adjacent to the cover, so they gain soft cover instead of hard cover.

**Example C:** As with B, but the smaller character

gains hard cover because they are

adjacent to the cover.

#### Bypassing Cover

You can ignore the effects of cover you are adjacent to, as long as you can aim over, through, or around said cover. You must be the same Size as or larger than the cover to aim over it. The GM has the final say on whether a character can bypass a specific piece of cover.

As a guideline, you can ignore a number of spaces of cover equal to your Size when aiming over, through, or around said cover.

#### **FIGURE 3:**

**Example A:** The Size 2 character can choose to bypass the cover because they can shoot over it. They still benefit from hard cover against attacks from the Size 1 character.

**Example B:** The Size 2 character can ignore the cover from the corner of the hallway because they can shoot around it. They still benefit from hard cover against attacks from the Size 1 character.

**Example C:** Both characters can shoot over the pillar because they are the same Size as it; however, due to its length, the GM decides they can't bypass it since they can't shoot over the entire length of the pillar.

### ATTACKS

#### **Attack roll summary: Roll 1d20 +** Grit **+ or**

Attacks are attempts by characters to hit and harm something with weapons, magic, or other means. To hit your target, your result must equal or exceed their defense (either Dodge or A-Def, depending on the type of attack).

Once an attack hits, any "**on-hit**" effects apply, with the attacker choosing the order when there are multiple such effects. Damage is the most common type of on-hit effect ([p. 58\)](#page-57-1), but many abilities have unique on-hit effects.

### ATTACK TYPES

Attacks can be either **melee attacks** or **ranged attacks**. They can be further categorized as **weapon**, **spell**, **support** or **skill** attacks, depending on their source.

Some abilities reference **reaction attacks**. These are attacks made with a **reaction**, like Counterattack.

All attacks have a Range or Reach (or both), which indicates the maximum number of spaces within which an attack can be made. Range and Reach are measured from the edge of one of the attacker's spaces. At least one of the target's spaces must be within an attack's Range or Reach to be targeted by the attack.

See pages [p. 53](#page-52-0) for more information on Range and Reach.

#### Melee Attacks

Melee attacks **ignore cover** ([p. 50\)](#page-49-0).

Most melee attacks have a Reach, which indicates the range of the attack. Reach is also used for certain actions like Opportunity Attack.

For example, the Dragon Rider's Longinus ability is a **melee weapon**. Therefore, attacks with it are considered melee weapon attacks. It has Reach 3, which means it can be used to attack enemies up to 3 spaces away.

#### **Longinus** Fight

Melee Weapon Heavy Longarm Reach 3 1d6+4 Physical

This weapon gains **Piercing** when attacking targets at its maximum Reach.

#### Ranged Attacks

Most ranged attacks have a Range, which indicates the range of the attack – the number of spaces within which it can be used to attack. Some ranged spell attacks list their Range as " Range = Scope ", which means that the Range is equal to the user's Scope.

For example, the Phoenix's Immolate ability is a ranged spell attack. Therefore, attacks with it are considered ranged spell attacks. It has a Range equal to the user's Scope, and can attack targets within that range.

**Immolate** Volley Ranged Spell Attack 1 Memory Aetheric, Indirect Range = Scope 2 Discord

**On Hit:** Choose one of the following:

- Ø **Building Fire:** If the target already has **Discord**, this attack deals 1d3+1 Discord instead of **2**.
- Ø **Spontaneous Combustion:** You can spend 1 MP to deal 1 Discord to all enemies in a Burst 1 AOE around the target.

Some ranged attacks also have a Reach, but they are still considered ranged attacks. Reach, in these cases, is used for certain actions like Opportunity Attack, but doesn't change the attack to a melee attack.

#### ATTACKS VS. ATTACK ROLLS

An attack can be composed of either a single attack roll, or in the case of abilities that affect an area, multiple attack rolls.

When an ability refers to an **attack**, it means all of the attack rolls made as part of that attack.

Anything that mentions an **attack roll** references a single roll, even if the attack involves multiple attack rolls.

#### AUTO-HIT

Certain abilities **auto-hit** their targets, meaning they automatically hit with no attack roll. Abilities with an auto-hit are considered attacks even though the attack roll doesn't take place. Interpose can still be used against auto-hit effects.

Not every ability that deals automatic damage is an attack – only those that specifically state they auto-hit count as attacks.

### CRITICAL HITS

On a result of 20+, an attack roll is a **crit**. Roll all damage dice twice and take the highest result.

Critical hits do more damage than normal hits. Critical hits only affect damage rolls, not Stress or static damage (e.g., 2 Physical ).

For example, if you critically hit with an attack that deals 2d6+2 Physical , you would roll 4d6, take the highest two, and add +2. If you rolled 4, 2, 6, and 3, you would use the 4 and 6.

### REROLL TABLE

When making an attack, ability check, or save **in combat**, you can reroll the dice by using the **Reroll Table**. To use the table, you choose one of the listed options and reroll all dice for one roll (including or ). Each option from the table can only be chosen once per round.

You can choose to reroll a dice after rolling, but before it is resolved as a failure or a success. For example, rerolling to turn a missed attack into a hit doesn't mean that you actually missed the attack on the first try. As such, it doesn't prompt any effects that trigger on misses (like **Reliable** damage).

### **REROLL TABLE** STRAIN You take **1d3 Stress**. ENDANGER You become Marked. You can't use this option if you are Marked. This doesn't count as a condition that you've given yourself. OVEREXTEND You can only choose this option if you are adjacent to an enemy or within Reach of an enemy's weapon. The enemy can choose one of the following, which resolves before you reroll: Ø They make an Opportunity Attack against you. Ø They deal 3 Physical to you as a **reaction**

### <span id="page-52-0"></span>RANGE, REACH, AND AREA OF EFFECT

Many abilities have a Range or Reach, which indicates the maximum number of spaces within which it can be used. Range and Reach work in the same way, but some effects trigger based on one or the other, not both.

Range and Reach are measured from the edge of one space to another. A target must have at least one of its spaces within an ability's Range (or Reach) to be targeted by the ability.

When an ability originates from a source larger than 1 space – a Size 2 character, for instance – the character using the ability decides which space to use as the point of origin.

For example, two adjacent characters are within 1 space of each other, which means any ability with Range 1 or greater could target them.

#### AREA OF EFFECT

Abilities and effects with an **area of effect** (AOE) affect multiple targets or spaces.

Abilities and effects with an AOE affect all targets within the indicated area. This area is the AOE. When making an AOE attack, you make **attack rolls against each target in the area**. **Damage, on the other hand, is only rolled once**, and applied to every affected target.

There are several different types of AOE, including **Line** (which affects all targets in a straight line) and **Blast** (which affects all targets within a certain radius).

If an effect lists a Range/Reach *and* an AOE, you can place the AOE's point of origin anywhere within the indicated Range/Reach. For example, if you have a Range 8, Line 5 effect, you can draw the Line 5 from any point within Range 8 and pointed in any direction.

#### <span id="page-52-1"></span>Zones

Zones are persistent AOEs that have ongoing effects. For example, at rank 1, the **Leyline Walker** talent allows a character to create Blast 1 **zones** that last until the end of the combat scene. Their AOE is Blast 1 , but because they're zones the effect is ongoing.

#### Standard Areas of Effect

**Blast X:** Affects all targets within a radius of X spaces from the center space, **including the center space**. Cover and line of sight are calculated from the center space. Users can be affected by their own **Blast**.

#### **Close Blast X:** As a **Blast**, but

- Ø at least 1 space must be adjacent to the user
- Ø the user can't be in the area
- Ø cover and line of sight are calculated from the user.

**Burst X:** Affects all targets within a radius of X spaces from the edge of the spaces occupied by the target, including the target's spaces but excluding the target itself. Cover and line of sight are calculated from the edge of the spaces occupied by the target. If a **Burst** forms a **zone**, the zone moves with the target.

**Line X:** Affects all targets in a straight line with a length of X spaces and a width of 1 space, drawn directly away from you in any direction.

### <span id="page-53-0"></span>MOVEMENT

Characters can move around the battlefield in various ways, including with their standard movement, or using special types of movement like **flight** and **teleportation**.

On your character's turn, you can use their **standard movement** to move a number of spaces equal to their Speed. Certain abilities can also move characters at other times.

Most of the time, entering an adjacent space counts as moving 1 space. This is its movement cost. But some effects, like **difficult terrain** [\(p. 56](#page-55-1)), can change the movement cost required to enter a space. Moving into these spaces requires characters to spend more of their total available movement. If a character doesn't have enough movement to enter a space, they can't move into that space.

Characters can move in any direction, even diagonally, and can move into any adjacent space that isn't **obstructed** or **impassable** (see below). Movement is always made on the horizontal plane; characters can't move vertically up or down.

To be considered moving, a character must move at least 1 space. Standing up from Prone doesn't count as movement.

Movement can be **voluntary** or **involuntary**. Voluntary movement is any movement that a character willingly takes. For example, a character taking their standard movement would consider the movement voluntary. Involuntary movement is not willingly taken by the character moving and is usually forced upon them, like forced movement [\(p. 55\)](#page-54-0).

#### LARGE CHARACTERS

Characters of Size 2+ occupy multiple spaces, and therefore enter more than 1 space when moving. When a Size 2+ character moves into multiple spaces with different movement costs, use the highest cost. For example, a Size 2 orc occupies a 2 × 2 space on the battlefield. They want to move forward 1 space, which involves moving into two new spaces. One of these spaces is **difficult terrain**, which costs 2 spaces of movement to enter, and the other is a normal space that costs 1. The orc would have to spend 2 spaces of movement to move forward.

### SPLITTING UP MOVEMENT

Voluntary movements can be split into any number of smaller movements punctuated by actions. Involuntary movements cannot.

For example, a character with Speed 6 could, when performing their **standard movement**, move 2 spaces, take an action, and then move 4 spaces.

If you can make multiple attacks with one action, they can move in between each attack.

*Example:* Dmitri wants to Full Attack an enemy 3 spaces away. He has Speed 6, so he uses his *standard movement*, moving 2 spaces so that he is adjacent to the enemy. His first attack is with his club, and with a lucky damage roll he defeats that enemy! He still has one more attack he can make from the Full Attack action, and 4 more spaces of movement left from his standard movement. There's another enemy 4 spaces away, so he uses the rest of his movement to get to that enemy and make his second attack.

#### SPECIAL MOVEMENT

Some forms of movement, like **flight**, have special rules. You only benefit from these special forms of movement if an ability or effect explicitly says so.

#### SHIFTING

A **shift** is a special movement granted by some abilities that doesn't provoke **reactions** and ignores Engagement.

You can't shift if you are Slowed or Immobilized.

#### TELEPORTATION

**Teleportation** is a special movement in which a character instantly travels between two spaces.

Teleportation does not require line of sight, doesn't provoke **reactions**, and ignores Engagement.

Teleportation counts as movement, and always counts as moving 1 space, regardless of the distance traveled. Immobilized characters can't teleport themselves, since they can't perform any movement.

If you try to teleport into a space you can't occupy, like impassable terrain or another character, the teleport fails and the action is lost.

#### FLIGHT

Characters with **flight** have the ability to fight on the ground or in the air.

Characters with flight can can either take off, gaining the Flying status, or land on the ground, clearing the status, as a **free action** once per turn. They can also choose to start combat scenes Flying.

Flight can be continuous, like with the draken ancestry trait Draconic Wings, or temporary, like with the Dragon Rider's ability Dragon Saddle. Abilities that say to fly a certain number of spaces mean to move that number of spaces as if under the effects of the Flying status.

While a character is Flying, the following effects apply:

- Ø Non-thrown melee attacks from non-Flying characters gain **+2** against them.
- Ø They can move normally over and are **immune** to the effects of terrain, obstructions, and terrain features, but cannot benefit from or interact with them either. When flying over objects, they are considered to have their Size increased by the Size of whatever they are flying over for the purposes of determining line of sight and cover, as if they were **climbing**.
- Ø They cannot benefit from cover from terrain or objects.
- Ø When attacking non-Flying characters, they treat their target's cover from terrain and objects as one step lower (hard cover becomes soft cover, soft cover becomes no cover).
- Ø They don't trigger **Traps** but can still be affected by them.
- Ø They ignore Engagement and don't cause Engagement.
- Ø They cease to be Flying if they become Prone.

#### CLIMBING

**Climbing** is a special movement that allows characters to move atop objects such as terrain.

Climbing has a movement cost equal to the object's Size + 1. For example, climbing onto a Size 1 crate costs 2 spaces worth of movement, while climbing atop a Size 4 wall costs 5 spaces of movement.

You cannot partially climb an object. If you can't move enough spaces to reach the top, you can't climb.

Moving from the top of an object to lower ground doesn't count as climbing.

Characters standing atop objects are considered to have their Size increased by the Size of whatever they are standing on for the purposes of determining line of sight and cover.

In BEACON, climbing is just a way to move. It doesn't provide any direct offensive or defensive benefits.

**High ground**, a special type of terrain, provides the benefits one might associate with having a height advantage. See **Terrain Features** ([p. 57](#page-56-0)) for more information.

#### SQUEEZING

**Squeezing** is a special movement that allows characters to fit into smaller spaces.

You can squeeze into spaces one Size smaller than you, but while in such a space, the cost of all movements is increased by +1.

#### <span id="page-54-0"></span>FORCED MOVEMENT

**Forced movement** is any involuntary movement that **pushes**, **pulls**, or causes a character to **slide**.

Each type of forced movement has its own rules:

- Ø **Push:** Characters and objects that are **pushed** move in a straight line directly away from the point of origin.
- Ø **Pull:** Characters and objects that are **pulled** are drawn in a straight line directly towards the point of origin.
- Ø **Slide:** Characters and objects that **slide** can move in any direction, chosen by the one causing the forced movement.

Forced movements do not provoke **reactions** and ignore Engagement. You can't be forcibly moved into a space you can't enter. For example, if there is a space only reachable by Flying units, a non-Flying character can't be pushed into it, but a Flying one could.

In some instances, you may be able to subject yourself to a push, pull, or slide effect. Even if you do so willingly, it still counts as involuntary movement.

#### OBSTRUCTION

Characters and objects block movement into the spaces they occupy. This is called **obstruction**.

Obstructions prevent movement if they are the same Size as the moving character or larger.

If an obstruction is an allied character or an enemy character smaller than you, they do not count as an obstruction and you can move through their spaces; however, you can't end a movement sharing spaces with another character.

If an obstruction is an object that is smaller than you, you can climb on it at no additional movement cost.

#### ENGAGEMENT

Characters gain the Engaged status when they are adjacent to one or more enemies. This is called Engagement.

When you become Engaged with an enemy of your Size or larger, your movement ends and you lose any unused movement. Once Engaged, you can begin new movements normally. Moving adjacent to a character with who you are already Engaged doesn't stop your movement, since you are still Engaged. The movement-ending effect only occurs when Engaged is applied.

While Engaged, you receive **+1** to all ranged attacks.

#### <span id="page-55-1"></span>DIFFICULT TERRAIN

Difficult terrain is anything that slows movement, like mud, water, or thick grass. **Difficult terrain** has +1 movement cost.

For example, if Dhalia has Speed 5, she can normally move 5 spaces. If she moves into a space of difficult terrain, it would cost 2 spaces worth of movement instead 1. She would only have 3 spaces of movement left, even though she only moved one space.

#### <span id="page-55-2"></span>DANGEROUS TERRAIN

**Dangerous terrain** is any type of terrain that can cause damage, like lava or thorns.

**1/round**, when you enter **dangerous terrain** or end your turn in it, you must make an ability check of the GM's choice. **Failure:** You take 5 damage of any type or Stress based on the type of terrain.

For example, if the ground is covered in freezing water, the GM might decide that it triggers a Bulk check and deals Lunar damage.

### ENVIRONMENTAL FACTORS

Environmental factors are effects that alter combat scenes, individual spaces, or even objects. Some apply to the entire combat scene, while others affect characters in smaller areas, such as in the case of a room with poor visibility.

This section lists some of the more common environmental factors. A full list of environmental factors can be found in **Chapter 10: Game Master's Guide** in the full version of BEACON.

#### <span id="page-55-0"></span>LOCKS

Doors, gates, and treasure chests can all be locked, preventing them from being opened. A locked entrance – a bolted door, for instance – can't be moved through. The contents of chests and other objects that are locked can't be accessed.

To unlock something, you can make an Agility check as part of the action to interact with it. On a success, the lock is disabled and the object is opened.

### LOW VISIBILITY

Low visibility can represent darkness, fog, and other effects that make it hard to see. It affects how far characters can see, and makes it harder to find Hidden characters. Low visibility has the following effects:

- Ø Characters are Invisible while Hidden.
- Ø Scope and the Range of all weapons are reduced by half. Reach and AOEs are not affected.

### UNDERWATER/ZERO-GRAVITY

Combat scenes that take place completely underwater or in zero-gravity treat all characters as Flying. However, unless they have **flight** or an **Aquatic Adaptation** (granted by certain abilities), they can't Rush or **shift**.

### ADAPTATIONS

Some abilities grant an adaptation to specific types of environments, like **Aquatic Adaptation**. Characters with an adaptation to an environment are **immune** to any of its negative effects. If it's not clear whether an effect is negative or not, the character with the adaptation can decide. Adaptations only affect the character with an adaptation. The **Low Visibility** environmental factor, for example, states that all characters are Invisible while Hidden. A character with an adaptation to that environment could decide that they don't want to be Invisible while Hidden, because that affects them directly, but they couldn't ignore the Invisible status on other Hidden characters, because that's an effect on other characters.

### <span id="page-56-0"></span>TERRAIN FEATURES

Terrain features modify spaces and terrain on the battlefield. While they generally affect single spaces, multiple terrain features can be placed next to each other to create a continuous feature. For example, you can have a single space of lava as dangerous terrain, but you could also connect multiple lava spaces to create a lava flow, all of which count as the same piece of dangerous terrain. **Cover** ([p. 50\)](#page-49-0), **difficult terrain** ([p. 56](#page-55-1)), and **dangerous terrain** [\(p. 56\)](#page-55-2) all count as terrain features.

This section lists some of the most common terrain features. A full list of terrain features can be found in **Chapter 10: Game Master's Guide** in the full version of BEACON.

#### EMPTY SPACE/FREE SPACE

A free space is a space that doesn't contain any characters or objects. Empty spaces, on the other hand, are more specific than free spaces. Empty spaces are those that don't contain any characters, objects, terrain, or terrain features.

Some abilities affect free spaces, while others will affect empty spaces only.

### HIGH GROUND

High ground provides benefits that represent being at a higher elevation than other characters, similar to those received when Flying. Characters standing on high ground gain the following benefits:

- Ø Non-thrown melee attacks against them from non-Flying characters that aren't on high ground receive **+2** .
- Ø They neither become Engaged by nor cause engagement to non-Flying characters that aren't on high ground.
- Ø Their attacks gain **+1** against non-Flying characters that aren't on high ground.

Accessing high ground sometimes requires special movement, like flying or climbing.

#### IMPASSABLE TERRAIN

Impassable terrain is any terrain, like a wall or a cliff, that cannot normally be traversed except by characters who possess a type of movement that allows them to do so, like flying or climbing. Some impassable terrain cannot be entered at all (e.g., a pillar that reaches up to the ceiling or the walls of an underground dungeon).

#### TRAPS

**Traps** are interactable **objects** with the **Trap** tag ([p.](#page-64-0)  [65](#page-64-0)). While many **Traps** are PC or NPC abilities, you can use these **Traps** to represent **Traps** already on the battlefield.

#### DAMAGE TRAP

The triggering character takes 6/8/10 damage of any type, chosen when the **Trap** is set.

#### AREA DAMAGE TRAP

When triggered, all characters in a Burst 1 AOE must make a save of the GM's choice. **Failure:** The target takes 6/8/10 damage of any type, chosen when the **Trap** is set. **Success:** They take 3/4/5 damage instead.

#### CONDITION TRAP

The triggering character gains a condition based on the type of **Trap**. Immobilized, Slowed, and Blinded are common.

#### AREA CONDITION TRAP

When triggered, all characters in a Burst 1 AOE must make a save of the GM's choice. **Failure:** They gain a condition, chosen when the **Trap** is set.

#### TREASURE CHESTS

Treasure chests are **objects** that don't cause obstruction and that contain bonus effects for the players, chosen from the table below. Players always know the contents of chests. The GM can decide if the bonuses apply to the character that opened the chest or everyone on their team. Some bonuses provide immediate effects, while others provide delayed effects or permanent resources.

Chests can be accessed with a **minor action** while in their space, but might be **locked** (see the **locked** environmental feature).

#### **EXAMPLE CHEST REWARDS**

- 1 A reroll on the next loot crate's first slot. Subsequent instances of this bonus increase the slot that can be rerolled. For example, getting this reward twice means that you can reroll either the first or second slot.
- 2 +X gold
- 3 +X Recoveries
- 4 Repair a destroyed weapon/support item/technique
- 5 A specific piece of loot
- 6 Regain X uses of a **Consumable** ability
- 7 Increase current and total MP by X for the rest of the scene
- 8 Clear all Stress

### <span id="page-57-1"></span>DAMAGE AND STRESS

Characters inflict harm on their enemies to wear them down and eventually defeat them. The most common sources of damage are attacks, but other abilities and effects can also inflict damage.

When you take damage, you subtract the damage taken from your current HP. For example, if you would take 5 Physical , your HP would be reduced by 5. Armor [\(p. 15\)](#page-14-0) and other effects can change the amount of damage you take. When your HP reaches 0, you take a **Wound** ([p. 60](#page-59-0)).

Stress is another type of harm separate from damage. Stress is accumulated until it exceeds your Stress Cap, at which point you take an **Overstress** [\(p. 60](#page-59-1)).

There are five standard types of damage – **Physical**, **Force**, **Astral**, **Lunar**, and **Discord**:

**Physical:** Damage from slashing, bludgeoning, and piercing weapons, and other physical sources.

**Force:** Damage caused by explosions and pure arcane energies.

**Astral:** Damage caused by heat, light, and other energy sources associated with the radiance of stars. Fire, light, and electricity are commonly associated with the Astral.

**Lunar:** Damage caused by cold, darkness, and other energy sources associated with the stillness of the moon. Ice, dark, and acid are commonly associated with the Lunar.

**Discord:** Damage that disrupts aetheric fields and puts its victims into a state of aetheric discord, like poison, burns, and blood loss, sapping the sufferer's lifeforce over time.

Physical, Force, Astral, and Lunar damage all function the same way, but certain abilities can make characters Vulnerable, **resistant**, or **immune** to certain damage types (see facing page).

**Discord** has additional rules associated with it, as it has a damage over time component (see below).

#### ORDER OF DAMAGE

To determine the final amount of damage dealt by any given action or effect, follow these steps in order:

- 1 Roll damage and bonus damage.
- 2 Halve bonus damage if there is more than one target.
- 3 Apply the effects of Weakened or Vulnerable, where relevant.
- 4 Subtract damage equal to the target's Armor.
- 5 If resistance applies, halve any relevant remaining damage.
- 6 Deal the remaining damage (rounded up) to the target.

#### DISCORD

Discord is a unique type of damage that deals **discord damage** to its victim's HP over time. It stacks with other Discord (see below) and is always **Piercing**.

**Discord** applies damage every turn. Whenever you take Discord, you immediately take **Piercing Discord damage** equal to the amount of Discord gained. You then make a note of the amount of Discord gained, adding it to any Discord you already have.

Make a Magic check at the end of each of your turns while you have Discord. **Success:** You clear all Discord. **Failure:** You take Discord damage equal to your current Discord.

Discord levels stacks. For example, if you have 2 Discord and gain another 2 Discord, you would have a total of 4 Discord.

Resistance and Vulnerable apply only to the upfront Discord you take (and, therefore, the amount of ongoing Discord you gain), not to the **discord damage**  you take if you fail the Magic check to clear it.

#### <span id="page-57-0"></span>STRESS

**Stress** is a special type of harm that doesn't count as damage, and doesn't affect the recipient's HP.

Instead of being subtracted from your HP, Stress is accumulated. When your Stress exceeds your Breaking point, you gain the Breaking status ([p. 63\)](#page-62-2), and when it exceeds your Stress Cap, you take an **Overstress** [\(p. 60](#page-59-1)).

Some characters (like **Summons**) don't have a Stress Cap. If these characters would take **Stress**, they instead take the same amount of **Force**.

Because **Stress** isn't damage, it ignores Armor and effects that modify damage don't modify **Stress** unless otherwise indicated. Taking **Stress** doesn't count as taking damage.

#### BONUS DAMAGE

**Bonus damage** is additional damage added onto an existing source of damage. Bonus damage is granted by abilities and effects.

Bonus damage is only ever added to **successful attacks**  or **abilities that deal damage**. It can only be Physical, Astral, Lunar, or Force damage (not Discord or Stress). Its type is the same as that of the underlying, basic damage, unless otherwise indicated. If an attack or ability normally deals multiple types of damage (e.g., Physical and Lunar), the character dealing the damage picks one for the bonus damage.

Effects with more than one target, like AOE attacks, only deal half their bonus damage to each target. Importantly, this applies only if there *is* more than one target, not if there *could be* multiple targets. If you attack with a Blast 1 explosion but there is only one target in the area, they take the full bonus damage. If there are two targets, the bonus damage would be halved.

Linked attacks don't deal bonus damage – only the first attack in the linked pair does.

#### VULNERABLE AND WEAKENED

The Vulnerable and Weakened conditions affect how much damage characters take and deal. Vulnerable characters take double damage, and Weakened characters deal half damage.

Modifications to damage from Vulnerable and Weakened happen before Armor and **resistances**. The full rules of these conditions can be found later in this chapter [\(p. 62](#page-61-2)).

*Example:* Dhalia is currently Weakened and uses a spell attack that deals 4 Astral . Her damage is reduced to 2 Astral because of her Weakened condition. If her target has 1 Armor, they would take a total of 1 Astral from Dhalia's attack.

Dmitri, on the other hand, is currently Vulnerable **to Lunar damage**. He triggers a *Trap* that explodes and deals 4 Lunar . Because he is Vulnerable to that damage type, he instead takes 8 Lunar . Dmitri then subtracts his 2 Armor, taking a total of 6 Lunar .

#### ARMOR, RESISTANCE, AND IMMUNITY

**Armor** reduces a set amount of damage, **resistance** halves damage, and **immunity** reduces damage to 0. Resistance and immunity often apply to specific types of damage.

**Armor** reduces incoming Physical, Astral, Lunar, and Force equal to its value. Armor does **not** reduce **Stress** or **Discord**. For example, 2 Armor would reduce Physical damage taken from an attack by 2.

Armor applies to each separate source of damage. For example, if you are hit by two attacks in a turn, Armor would apply to each attack.

**Resistance** halves any incoming damage of an indicated type, or even Stress. For example, if you would take 10 Astral and have **resistance to Astral**, you only take 5 Astral .

**Immunity** negates *all* incoming damage or Stress from whatever it is you have immunity against. For example, if you would take 4 Stress and have **Immunity to Stress**, you take no Stress at all.

#### OBJECT AND TERRAIN DAMAGE

Objects and pieces of terrain can take damage. By default, they have 10 HP per Size, 5 Dodge, and are **immune** to **Aetheric** attacks and Stress.

The GM may allow objects and terrain to be broken in smaller segments. For example, you might punch a hole through a wall instead of destroying the entire wall at once.

In some cases, the GM may allow for objects or terrain to be broken with successful a Bulk check. This might be the case if you try to ram down a wooden door.

In general, the ground does not take damage. This can be ignored in certain situations, like if you want to destroy the floor in a house to fall into the basement. But in most cases, no one needs to track how much damage the ground is taking.

#### <span id="page-59-0"></span>WOUNDS AND OVERSTRESS

In combat, characters can receive **Wounds** or become **Overstressed**. As they accumulate Wounds and Overstresses, their combat performance weakens until they are no longer able to fight.

#### Wounds

When a character reaches 0 HP, they take a **Wound** and make a **wound roll**.

When you reach 0 HP, you take a Wound and reset your HP, carrying over any excess damage. You then make a **wound roll** by rolling 1d6 and consulting the Wound Table to see what additional penalties you suffer.

Beacons have a Wound Cap of 4, while NPCs usually have different Wound Caps. When you have Wounds equal to your Wound Cap, you are **defeated**.

#### <span id="page-59-1"></span>Overstressed

When a PC takes Stress in excess of their Stress Cap, they take an **Overstress**. NPCs follow different rules when they exceed their Stress Cap.

When you take **Stress**, it accumulates until it **exceeds** your Stress Cap. If you go over your Stress Cap, you take an Overstress and reset your Stress, carrying over any excess Stress. If this causes you to exceed your Stress Cap a second time, you take another Overstress. When you receive an Overstress, you also make an **overstress roll** by rolling 1d6 and consulting the Overstress Table to see what additional penalties you suffer.

Beacons have an Overstress Cap of 4, while NPCs usually have different Overstress Caps. When you have Overstresses equal to your Overstress Cap, you are **defeated**.
